MYCIN: The Rule-Based Doctor of the 1970s JAH-ESYS-000001 · 1

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

MYCIN was the expert system that made the field famous: a program that reasoned about bacterial infections the way a specialist would, asking the doctor questions, chaining through hundreds of rules, and recommending a therapy with a certainty factor attached. It never treated a real patient, yet its design set the pattern for every rule-based system that followed: a knowledge base of if-then rules kept separate from the inference engine that applied them, an explanation facility that could say why it asked each question, and a way of combining uncertain evidence into a single number. This record gives its dated history, its method, and a worked example of its certainty-factor arithmetic.

History

MYCIN was built at Stanford University between 1972 and 1980, led by Edward Shortliffe as his doctoral work under Bruce Buchanan, with Randall Davis and others contributing. It diagnosed bacterial infections of the blood and meningitis and recommended antibiotic therapy, drawing on a knowledge base of about 600 rules. In a blinded 1979 evaluation, its therapy recommendations were judged acceptable by independent experts in 65 percent of cases, against 42.5 to 62.5 percent for the human specialists tested. Despite that result it was never used in routine clinical practice, held back by legal and ethical questions about a machine prescribing drugs, by the effort of entering patient data, and by the fact that it could not examine a patient. Its ideas lived on: the certainty-factor model, the separation of knowledge from inference, and the explanation facility all became standard parts of the expert-system pattern.

Description

MYCIN was the expert system that made the field famous: a program that reasoned about bacterial infections the way a specialist would, asking the doctor questions, chaining through hundreds of rules, and recommending a therapy with a certainty factor attached. It never treated a real patient, yet its design set the pattern for every rule-based system that followed: a knowledge base of if-then rules kept separate from the inference engine that applied them, an explanation facility that could say why it asked each question, and a way of combining uncertain evidence into a single number. This record gives its dated history, its method, and a worked example of its certainty-factor arithmetic.

The Problem

What is the MYCIN expert system for bacterial infection diagnosis and therapy, built at Stanford in the 1970s, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the patient's facts as evidence, each with a certainty factor between -1 and 1. Step 2: match the facts against the rule base; a rule fires when its premises are satisfied, and its conclusion inherits a certainty factor from the rule and the evidence. Step 3: combine the certainty factors of independent pieces of supporting evidence with the MYCIN rule, CF combined equals CF1 plus CF2 minus CF1 times CF2, so two moderately strong pieces of evidence add up to a stronger conclusion than either alone. Step 4: chain the fired rules forward until a therapy recommendation is reached, then report the recommendation with its combined certainty factor and the chain of rules behind it. The demo runs a small worked example of this combination step: it combines three pairs of certainty factors, 0.8 with 0.6, 0.7 with 0.5, and 0.9 with 0.4, and reports each combined value and their mean. The demo is a worked example of the method, not a record of a historical consultation. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"rules_about": 600, "evaluation_acceptability_percent": 65, "specialist_low_percent": 42.5, "specialist_high_percent": 62.5, "built_start": 1972, "built_end": 1980}.

The Answer

The 1979 evaluation is the checkable headline: MYCIN's recommendations were judged acceptable in 65 percent of cases, against 42.5 to 62.5 percent for the human specialists in the same test. The knowledge base held about 600 rules, and the system covered bacterial infections of the blood and meningitis. The worked example shows the combination arithmetic doing what the design claims: 0.8 and 0.6 combine to 0.92, 0.7 and 0.5 combine to 0.85, and 0.9 and 0.4 combine to 0.94, for a mean combined certainty of 0.9033. The result that matters most is the negative one: a system can outscore specialists on paper and still never reach the clinic, because acceptance, liability, and workflow matter as much as accuracy. The demo prints these checked figures: mean_combined: 0.9033, n: 3.

When It Applies

MYCIN applies wherever a narrow domain can be written down as rules and the stakes allow a machine to advise rather than decide: diagnosis support, fault finding, configuration, and any task where an expert's judgment follows recognizable patterns. It applies as an adviser that shows its reasoning, not as an autonomous decider, and only where a human expert can check its chain of rules. It does not apply where the rules cannot be written, where the domain changes faster than the rules can be maintained, or where the cost of a wrong recommendation falls on someone who cannot question it.

Limits

The limits are plain and were visible at the time. MYCIN could not learn: every rule had to be written by a human, so the knowledge base froze the moment its authors stopped work. It could not handle a case outside its rules, and it had no common sense to notice when its inputs were wrong. It needed a human to type in every finding, which made it slower than the doctor it was meant to help. Its certainty factors were not probabilities, so they could not be combined with real statistical evidence. And the 1979 evaluation, strong as it was, tested therapy choice on paper cases, not outcomes in real patients, so it proved the system could reason like a specialist, not that it could care for one.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the certainty-factor combination rule. For two positive factors the combined value is CF1 plus CF2 minus their product, which keeps the result below 1, grows with each new piece of evidence, and gives diminishing returns as the total approaches certainty. With 0.8 and 0.6 the product is 0.48 and the sum is 1.4, so the combined value is 0.92. Repeating the rule over the three worked pairs gives 0.92, 0.85, and 0.94, whose mean is 0.9033. The rule is associative in the sense that the order of combination does not change the result, which is what lets a long chain of rules accumulate evidence without depending on the order the rules happen to fire. Formal statement: CF_combined = CF1 + CF2 - CF1 * CF2, for two positive certainty factors CF1 and CF2; mean combined = (sum of combined values) / n. The equation states the certainty-factor check the demo performs: it combines each pair of positive certainty factors by the MYCIN rule and averages the combined values.

Science Proof

The science behind MYCIN is the science of heuristic reasoning under uncertainty: a doctor rarely has certain evidence, so the system must carry degrees of belief and combine them without pretending to be a probability calculus. Certainty factors were a pragmatic compromise, defined operationally by how they combined, and the 1979 evaluation tested them against the judgment of real specialists rather than against a formal standard. The system worked because the domain was narrow: bacterial infections have a bounded set of organisms, a bounded set of drugs, and a literature that experts could turn into rules. Outside that narrow scope the same approach had nothing to say, which is why MYCIN's success did not transfer to general medicine. Scientific relation: A certainty factor states how strongly evidence supports a conclusion, from -1 for certain disbelief to 1 for certain belief; two pieces of supporting evidence combine to a stronger factor than either alone.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
cfs = [(0.8, 0.6), (0.7, 0.5), (0.9, 0.4)]
def compute():
    combined = [a + b - a * b for a, b in cfs]
    mean_combined = round(sum(combined) / len(combined), 4)
    n = len(cfs)
    out = {"mean_combined": mean_combined, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYS000001:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        cfs = [(0.8, 0.6), (0.7, 0.5), (0.9, 0.4)]
        def compute():
            combined = [a + b - a * b for a, b in cfs]
            mean_combined = round(sum(combined) / len(combined), 4)
            n = len(cfs)
            out = {"mean_combined": mean_combined, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYS000001().run())

DENDRAL: The First Expert System JAH-ESYS-000002 · 2

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

DENDRAL was the first expert system, and it proved the idea before the field had a name: a program that could take the mass spectrum of an unknown organic molecule and propose the molecule's structure, doing work that took a skilled chemist hours. It mattered because it showed that expert knowledge could be captured as rules and heuristics and then run by a machine, and because it taught its builders how hard that capture is. The program worked in three stages, planning what to look for, generating candidate structures, and testing them against the spectrum, and its success launched the knowledge-engineering enterprise that produced MYCIN and everything after it.

History

DENDRAL began at Stanford in 1965, led by Edward Feigenbaum with Joshua Lederberg, Bruce Buchanan, and Carl Djerassi. Lederberg, a Nobel laureate geneticist, wanted a program that could reason about molecular structure, and the mass spectrometer gave the problem a clean input: a spectrum of peaks that constrains what the molecule can be. The plan-generate-test design became the template for expert systems: the planner used constraints from the spectrum, the generator enumerated candidate structures consistent with those constraints, and the tester scored each candidate against the observed peaks. DENDRAL worked well enough on its test problems to convince its builders that the knowledge-based approach was real, and Feigenbaum carried the lesson into the expert-systems work of the 1970s. It remained a research system rather than a laboratory instrument, but its lineage runs straight through MYCIN to the commercial systems of the 1980s.

Description

DENDRAL was the first expert system, and it proved the idea before the field had a name: a program that could take the mass spectrum of an unknown organic molecule and propose the molecule's structure, doing work that took a skilled chemist hours. It mattered because it showed that expert knowledge could be captured as rules and heuristics and then run by a machine, and because it taught its builders how hard that capture is. The program worked in three stages, planning what to look for, generating candidate structures, and testing them against the spectrum, and its success launched the knowledge-engineering enterprise that produced MYCIN and everything after it.

The Problem

What is the DENDRAL expert system for molecular structure from mass spectra, built at Stanford from 1965, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the mass spectrum as input, a list of peaks with their masses and intensities. Step 2: plan, using heuristics about the compound class to constrain the search, so the generator does not enumerate every conceivable structure. Step 3: generate candidate structures consistent with the constraints. Step 4: test each candidate by predicting its spectrum and scoring the match against the observed peaks, then rank the candidates and report the best. The demo runs a small worked example of the test stage: it scores five candidate structures with match scores of 0.92, 0.78, 0.85, 0.61, and 0.95, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the scoring method, not a record of a historical DENDRAL run. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"started": 1965, "stages": 3, "best_score": 0.95, "mean_score": 0.822}.

The Answer

DENDRAL's result was a proof of concept rather than a product: on its test problems it generated the correct structure among its top-ranked candidates, which showed that heuristic knowledge could guide a search through a space far too large to enumerate blindly. The worked example shows the scoring arithmetic: the five candidate scores 0.92, 0.78, 0.85, 0.61, and 0.95 total 4.11, with a mean of 0.822, a smallest of 0.61, and a largest of 0.95. The lasting result was methodological: plan, generate, and test became the standard shape of a knowledge-based system, and the knowledge-acquisition problem DENDRAL exposed became the central difficulty of the field. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 0.95, mean: 0.822, n: 5, smallest: 0.61, total: 4.11.

When It Applies

DENDRAL applies wherever a hard measurement constrains a large search space and expert heuristics can prune it: structure elucidation, diagnosis from instrument readings, and any generate-and-test problem where the test is cheap and reliable. It applies as a design pattern, plan then generate then test, even where the domain is nothing like chemistry. It does not apply where the test is expensive, where the heuristics cannot be written down, or where the search space resists pruning.

Limits

The limits are the limits of the whole first generation. DENDRAL's knowledge was hand-built and narrow: it worked on the compound classes its authors had encoded and had nothing to say outside them. It could not learn from new spectra, so every improvement needed a chemist and a programmer. Its generator could still explode combinatorially on large molecules, and its test stage depended on a predictive model of spectra that was itself approximate. It was a research demonstration, not a laboratory tool, and the gap between the two is the gap between a system that works on chosen problems and one that works on whatever arrives.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of scored ranking. Five candidate scores are averaged by adding them and dividing by five: 0.92 plus 0.78 plus 0.85 plus 0.61 plus 0.95 equals 4.11, and 4.11 divided by 5 is 0.822. The best score is the maximum, 0.95, and the spread is the range from the minimum 0.61 to the maximum 0.95. Ranking by score is only as good as the scoring function, and DENDRAL's contribution was to show that a scoring function built from expert heuristics could rank the true structure first often enough to be useful. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind DENDRAL is the science of heuristic search: a molecule's structure space is astronomically large, so no program can enumerate it, and the only way forward is knowledge that prunes the search before it starts. DENDRAL's heuristics came from chemists, encoded as rules about what structures are plausible given a spectrum, and the test stage grounded those guesses in measurement. The system worked because mass spectrometry gives hard constraints: a candidate that predicts the wrong peaks is wrong, whatever the heuristics say. That grounding is what separated DENDRAL from pure speculation, and it is the reason the same pattern worked later in medicine and configuration, where the test is an expert's judgment rather than a spectrum. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. Step 3: generate candidate structures consistent with the constraints. Step 4: test each candidate by predicting its spectrum and scoring the match against the observed peaks, then rank the candidates and report the best. The demo runs a small worked example of the test stage: it scores five candidate structures with match scores of 0.92, 0.78, 0.85, 0.61, and 0.95, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the scoring method, not a record of a historical DENDRAL run.

Dataset

Dataset: DENDRAL, Stanford, from 1965; plan-generate-test design; three stages. Demo: worked example scoring five candidate structures, match scores 0.92, 0.78, 0.85, 0.61, 0.95. Provenance: public-harvest from the cited public sources; licensing: public facts and figures, freely reusable, with sources cited. Last verified 2026-11-16.

Formal equation

total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts

Formal equation, in plain words

The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.

Proof sketch

The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

DENDRAL's result was a proof of concept rather than a product: on its test problems it generated the correct structure among its top-ranked candidates, which showed that heuristic knowledge could guide a search through a space far too large to enumerate blindly. The worked example shows the scoring arithmetic: the five candidate scores 0.92, 0.78, 0.85, 0.61, and 0.95 total 4.11, with a mean of 0.822, a smallest of 0.61, and a largest of 0.95. The lasting result was methodological: plan, generate, and test became the standard shape of a knowledge-based system, and the knowledge-acquisition problem DENDRAL exposed became the central difficulty of the field. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 0.95, mean: 0.822, n: 5, smallest: 0.61, total: 4.11.

Skeptical analysis

The limits are the limits of the whole first generation. DENDRAL's knowledge was hand-built and narrow: it worked on the compound classes its authors had encoded and had nothing to say outside them. It could not learn from new spectra, so every improvement needed a chemist and a programmer. Its generator could still explode combinatorially on large molecules, and its test stage depended on a predictive model of spectra that was itself approximate. It was a research demonstration, not a laboratory tool, and the gap between the two is the gap between a system that works on chosen problems and one that works on whatever arrives.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('candidate 1', 0.92), ('candidate 2', 0.78), ('candidate 3', 0.85), ('candidate 4', 0.61), ('candidate 5', 0.95)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

XCON: The Expert System That Paid for Itself JAH-ESYS-000003 · 3

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

XCON, originally called R1, was the expert system that proved the technology could pay: a program that took a customer's order for a VAX computer and worked out exactly which components, cables, and cabinets were needed, in what order, and how they fit together. It replaced a slow, error-prone manual job, it ran thousands of times a year, and by the mid-1980s it was saving Digital Equipment Corporation about twenty-five million dollars a year. It was also the system that showed the other side of the bargain: its rule base grew to about two thousand five hundred rules, and keeping those rules correct became a job in itself.

History

XCON was built at Carnegie Mellon University by John McDermott and his team, working with Digital Equipment Corporation, and went into service in 1978 configuring VAX-11/780 systems. A VAX order was a combinatorial puzzle: processors, memory, disks, controllers, and cabinets had to be compatible, cabled correctly, and packed into the right number of cabinets, and a human configurator needed deep product knowledge to get it right. XCON encoded that knowledge as production rules and searched for a configuration that satisfied every constraint. By 1986 it was processing about 80,000 orders a year, its rule base had grown to about 2,500 rules, and DEC credited it with saving about 25 million dollars a year. It stayed in service for years and was extended to new product lines, becoming the standard example of a commercially successful expert system.

Description

XCON, originally called R1, was the expert system that proved the technology could pay: a program that took a customer's order for a VAX computer and worked out exactly which components, cables, and cabinets were needed, in what order, and how they fit together. It replaced a slow, error-prone manual job, it ran thousands of times a year, and by the mid-1980s it was saving Digital Equipment Corporation about twenty-five million dollars a year. It was also the system that showed the other side of the bargain: its rule base grew to about two thousand five hundred rules, and keeping those rules correct became a job in itself.

The Problem

What is the XCON expert system that configured VAX computers for Digital Equipment Corporation from 1978, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the order as a set of components the customer asked for. Step 2: match the configuration rules against the order, adding required components, choosing cabinets, and assigning slots. Step 3: check every constraint, including compatibility, power, and cabling, and backtrack when a choice violates one. Step 4: emit the full configuration, the parts list, and the assembly instructions. The demo runs a small worked example of the scoring step: it compares five orders by their component counts and rules checked, with pairs of 12 and 14, 8 and 9, 20 and 22, 15 and 16, and 10 and 11, and reports the average gap between the two counts and the largest gap. The demo is a worked example of the checking arithmetic, not a record of a historical XCON run. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"in_service": 1978, "orders_per_year_about": 80000, "rules_about": 2500, "saving_dollars_millions_about": 25}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the scale figures: about 80,000 orders a year by 1986, about 2,500 rules in the knowledge base, and about 25 million dollars a year saved. The worked example shows the checking arithmetic on five small orders: the gaps between the paired counts are 2, 1, 2, 1, and 1, giving a mean absolute error of 1.4 and a root mean square error of 1.4832, with a largest gap of 2. The result that matters is the economic one: XCON was the first expert system whose value could be counted in dollars, and that count is what carried the field into its commercial boom. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 1.4, max_err: 2, n: 5, rmse: 1.4832.

When It Applies

XCON applies wherever a product is assembled from a closed catalogue under known compatibility rules: computer configuration, industrial equipment, vehicles, and any made-to-order product with a parts list. It applies where the catalogue changes slowly enough for the rules to keep up, and where the cost of a wrong configuration is high enough to justify the maintenance. It does not apply where the catalogue is open-ended, where compatibility is a matter of judgment rather than rules, or where the product changes faster than the rule base can be revised.

Limits

The limits showed up as success. Every new product meant new rules, and the rule base grew until no single person understood it all; a change in one rule could break a configuration somewhere else, and finding the break took expert effort. XCON's maintenance cost became the cautionary tale of the field: a rule base is an asset that decays, and the decay is fastest exactly when the product line changes fastest. The system also knew nothing outside its catalogue, so a customer asking for something new got no help at all. Its success proved the technology and its maintenance bill proved the cost.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five paired counts differ by 2, 1, 2, 1, and 1, so the mean absolute error is 7 divided by 5, which is 1.4. The squared errors are 4, 1, 4, 1, and 1, which sum to 11, so the mean squared error is 2.2 and the root mean square error is the square root of 2.2, which is 1.4832. The largest single gap is 2. These are small numbers because the worked example is small; the point is the method, which scales to any number of orders without change. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind XCON is the science of constraint satisfaction: a configuration is a solution to a large set of interlocking constraints, and the knowledge that matters is which combinations are allowed. XCON's rules carried that knowledge in a form a machine could apply exhaustively and consistently, which is exactly what humans find hard at scale. The system worked because the domain was closed: DEC's product line was finite, its compatibility rules were documented, and every order was a combination of known parts. Outside a closed catalogue the same approach has nothing to grip, which is why configuration systems succeeded in computing and manufacturing and struggled everywhere else. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(12, 14), (8, 9), (20, 22), (15, 16), (10, 11)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

PROSPECTOR: The Expert System That Found a Mine JAH-ESYS-000004 · 4

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

PROSPECTOR was the expert system that found something real: a program that reasoned about geology the way an exploration geologist would, weighing rock types, alteration patterns, and assay data to judge where a mineral deposit might lie. In 1979 it pointed at a site on Mount Tolman in Washington State and said a molybdenum deposit was probably there, and drilling found one, reported at the time as worth on the order of one hundred million dollars. The deposit had already been noticed by geologists, which keeps the story honest, but PROSPECTOR's prediction was made from its own reasoning, and it remains the most famous single prediction in the history of expert systems.

History

PROSPECTOR was built at SRI International in the 1970s by Richard Duda, Peter Hart, and Nils Nilsson, with geologists advising. It used a Bayesian inference network: geological evidence was linked to hypotheses about deposits, and each piece of evidence moved the probability up or down. The knowledge base covered the geology of porphyry molybdenum and copper deposits, with rules about the rock types, the alteration zones, and the geochemical signatures that indicate ore. In 1979 the system evaluated the Mount Tolman site and gave a high probability for a molybdenum deposit; drilling confirmed a deposit, and the find was reported at the time as worth on the order of one hundred million dollars. The case is always told with the caveat that geologists had already flagged the site, so PROSPECTOR confirmed and quantified a suspicion rather than discovering a deposit from nothing. That caveat is part of the record, not a footnote to hide.

Description

PROSPECTOR was the expert system that found something real: a program that reasoned about geology the way an exploration geologist would, weighing rock types, alteration patterns, and assay data to judge where a mineral deposit might lie. In 1979 it pointed at a site on Mount Tolman in Washington State and said a molybdenum deposit was probably there, and drilling found one, reported at the time as worth on the order of one hundred million dollars. The deposit had already been noticed by geologists, which keeps the story honest, but PROSPECTOR's prediction was made from its own reasoning, and it remains the most famous single prediction in the history of expert systems.

The Problem

What is the PROSPECTOR expert system for mineral exploration, built at SRI International in the 1970s, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the geological evidence for a site, rock type, alteration, assays, and structure. Step 2: enter the evidence into the inference network, where each fact updates the probability of the deposit hypotheses. Step 3: propagate the updates through the network, combining independent evidence by the Bayesian rules, until every hypothesis has a posterior probability. Step 4: report the most probable deposit type with its probability and the evidence that moved it. The demo runs a small worked example of the probability step: it takes five predicted probabilities, 0.85, 0.6, 0.9, 0.45, and 0.75, against outcomes of 1, 1, 1, 0, and 1, and reports the Brier score and the mean predicted probability. The demo is a worked example of the calibration arithmetic, not a record of the Mount Tolman evaluation. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"prediction_year": 1979, "deposit_value_dollars_millions_about": 100, "brier_worked": 0.0915, "mean_p_worked": 0.71}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the 1979 prediction and its confirmation: PROSPECTOR gave a high probability for molybdenum at Mount Tolman, drilling found a deposit, and the find was reported at the time as worth on the order of one hundred million dollars. The worked example shows the calibration arithmetic: the Brier score of the five predictions is 0.0915 and the mean predicted probability is 0.71, with 5 hits out of 5. The result that matters is methodological: a Bayesian network could turn scattered geological evidence into a quantified judgment, and the judgment could be tested by drilling, which is the strongest kind of test a prediction can face. The demo prints these checked figures: brier: 0.0915, hits: 5, mean_p: 0.71, n: 5.

When It Applies

PROSPECTOR applies wherever evidence must be combined into a quantified judgment and the judgment can be tested: mineral exploration, medical diagnosis, fault finding, and any domain where the evidence is observable and the hypotheses are enumerable. It applies where the probabilities can be grounded in frequencies or in expert judgment that can be checked, and where the cost of testing the prediction is worth paying. It does not apply where the evidence cannot be quantified, where the hypotheses are open-ended, or where a single dramatic success is mistaken for a validated system.

Limits

The limits are the limits of any Bayesian system that depends on its priors and its evidence. PROSPECTOR's network covered the deposit types its authors had encoded, and a deposit of an unmodeled type was invisible to it. Its probabilities depended on the experts' judgments about how strongly each kind of evidence points to ore, and those judgments were estimates, not measurements. The Mount Tolman story is also a single case, and a single confirmed prediction does not prove a system, however dramatic it is. PROSPECTOR's real contribution was to show that probabilistic reasoning could be built into an expert system at all, not that one prediction settled the question.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the Brier score. The five predictions and outcomes give squared gaps of 0.0225, 0.16, 0.01, 0.2025, and 0.0625, which sum to 0.4575, and 0.4575 divided by 5 is 0.0915. The mean predicted probability is 3.55 divided by 5, which is 0.71, and 5 of the 5 predictions were hits. A Brier score near zero means the probabilities were well calibrated; a score near 0.25 means they were no better than guessing. The worked example's score of 0.0915 sits between the two, which is the honest place for a system that is usually right and sometimes wrong. Formal statement: Brier = (1/n) * sum (p_i - o_i)^2, where p_i is the predicted probability and o_i is 1 when the event happened and 0 when it did not. The equation states the calibration check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted probabilities and outcomes, squares each gap, and averages them for the Brier score.

Science Proof

The science behind PROSPECTOR is the science of reasoning under uncertainty with numbers that mean something: its probabilities were probabilities, not certainty factors, so they could be combined by Bayes' rule and checked against frequencies. The system worked because geology gives the right kind of evidence: rock types and alteration zones are observable facts, and their association with deposits is a matter of record. The network made the reasoning explicit, so a geologist could see which evidence moved the probability and by how much, and could argue with the system rather than merely accept it. That transparency is what made the prediction credible before the drilling confirmed it. Scientific relation: A predicted probability is calibrated when events it calls likely actually happen often; the Brier score measures that gap, with 0 for perfect prediction.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

History

PROSPECTOR was built at SRI International in the 1970s by Richard Duda, Peter Hart, and Nils Nilsson, with geologists advising. It used a Bayesian inference network: geological evidence was linked to hypotheses about deposits, and each piece of evidence moved the probability up or down. The knowledge base covered the geology of porphyry molybdenum and copper deposits, with rules about the rock types, the alteration zones, and the geochemical signatures that indicate ore. In 1979 the system evaluated the Mount Tolman site and gave a high probability for a molybdenum deposit; drilling confirmed a deposit, and the find was reported at the time as worth on the order of one hundred million dollars. The case is always told with the caveat that geologists had already flagged the site, so PROSPECTOR confirmed and quantified a suspicion rather than discovering a deposit from nothing. That caveat is part of the record, not a footnote to hide.

Problem

What is the PROSPECTOR expert system for mineral exploration, built at SRI International in the 1970s, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

Method

Step 1: take the geological evidence for a site, rock type, alteration, assays, and structure. Step 2: enter the evidence into the inference network, where each fact updates the probability of the deposit hypotheses. Step 3: propagate the updates through the network, combining independent evidence by the Bayesian rules, until every hypothesis has a posterior probability. Step 4: report the most probable deposit type with its probability and the evidence that moved it. The demo runs a small worked example of the probability step: it takes five predicted probabilities, 0.85, 0.6, 0.9, 0.45, and 0.75, against outcomes of 1, 1, 1, 0, and 1, and reports the Brier score and the mean predicted probability. The demo is a worked example of the calibration arithmetic, not a record of the Mount Tolman evaluation.

Dataset

Working demo program

import hashlib
probs = [(0.85, 1), (0.6, 1), (0.9, 1), (0.45, 0), (0.75, 1)]
def compute():
    brier = round(sum((p - o) ** 2 for p, o in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    hits = sum(1 for p, o in probs if (p >= 0.5) == bool(o))
    n = len(probs)
    out = {"brier": brier, "mean_p": mean_p, "hits": hits, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))

        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()

            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")

PUFF and CADUCEUS: Expert Systems in Medicine JAH-ESYS-000005 · 5

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

After MYCIN, medicine became the proving ground for expert systems, and two systems show the range: PUFF, a small, practical program that interpreted pulmonary function tests and actually ran in a hospital, and CADUCEUS, a large, ambitious program that tried to cover the whole of internal medicine. PUFF had a few dozen rules and a single job, and it did that job well enough to be used. CADUCEUS aimed at hundreds of diseases and the tangled relationships between them, and it showed how hard the large version of the problem is. Together they mark the two poles of medical expert systems: the narrow tool that works and the broad system that teaches.

History

PUFF was built at Stanford in 1979 by John Kunz and colleagues, as a direct descendant of MYCIN: it took the results of pulmonary function tests, applied about fifty rules, and produced an interpretation of the patient's lung function. It ran at the Pacific Medical Center in San Francisco and was used in routine practice, which made it one of the first expert systems to do real clinical work rather than demonstration cases. CADUCEUS, also called INTERNIST-II, was built at the University of Pittsburgh in the 1980s by Harry Pople and Randolph Miller, building on INTERNIST-I, Jack Myers' earlier system from the 1970s. INTERNIST-I covered hundreds of diseases with thousands of manifestations; CADUCEUS added causal reasoning about how diseases produce findings and how several diseases can be present at once. It remained a research system, and its complexity became a lesson in itself: covering internal medicine meant covering the interactions between diseases, and those interactions grew faster than the rule base.

Description

After MYCIN, medicine became the proving ground for expert systems, and two systems show the range: PUFF, a small, practical program that interpreted pulmonary function tests and actually ran in a hospital, and CADUCEUS, a large, ambitious program that tried to cover the whole of internal medicine. PUFF had a few dozen rules and a single job, and it did that job well enough to be used. CADUCEUS aimed at hundreds of diseases and the tangled relationships between them, and it showed how hard the large version of the problem is. Together they mark the two poles of medical expert systems: the narrow tool that works and the broad system that teaches.

The Problem

What is the PUFF and CADUCEUS expert systems for pulmonary and internal-medicine diagnosis in the late 1970s and 1980s, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the clinical findings as input, test results and symptoms with their values. Step 2: match the findings against the disease knowledge base, scoring each disease by how well the findings fit. Step 3: for CADUCEUS-style reasoning, consider combinations of diseases and their causal links, and prefer the explanation that accounts for the most findings with the fewest diseases. Step 4: report the leading diagnosis with its supporting findings and the alternatives that were considered. The demo runs a small worked example of the scoring step: it takes five diseases with fit scores of 0.88, 0.72, 0.91, 0.55, and 0.79 against reference scores of 0.9, 0.7, 0.9, 0.6, and 0.8, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the scoring arithmetic, not a record of a historical consultation. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"puff_year": 1979, "puff_rules_about": 50, "diseases_hundreds": true, "mae_worked": 0.022, "rmse_worked": 0.0265}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the deployment facts: PUFF ran in routine use at the Pacific Medical Center from 1979, built on about fifty rules, while INTERNIST-I and CADUCEUS covered hundreds of diseases and thousands of manifestations as research systems. The worked example shows the scoring arithmetic: the gaps between the fit scores and their references are 0.02, 0.02, 0.01, 0.05, and 0.01, giving a mean absolute error of 0.022 and a root mean square error of 0.0265, with a largest gap of 0.05. The result that matters is the contrast: the narrow system reached the clinic and the broad system stayed in the laboratory, and that contrast has repeated in medical AI ever since. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.022, max_err: 0.05, n: 5, rmse: 0.0265.

When It Applies

PUFF and CADUCEUS apply as a pair of lessons: build narrow diagnostic tools that do one job completely, and treat broad diagnostic systems as research instruments until their evaluation is as strong as their ambition. They apply wherever a test result has a small set of interpretations and the rules can be written and checked by specialists. They do not apply as general diagnosticians, they do not apply where the findings cannot be entered reliably, and they do not apply where a wrong ranking would be acted on without a clinician checking it.

Limits

The limits are the limits of hand-built medical knowledge. PUFF's fifty rules covered pulmonary function and nothing else, so a patient with a second problem got no help with it. CADUCEUS's ambition ran into the combinatorial reality of multiple diseases, and its causal reasoning was never fast or complete enough for the clinic. Both systems needed their findings entered by hand, both froze when their authors stopped maintaining them, and neither could learn from the cases it saw. Their lesson is durable: a medical system succeeds when its domain is narrow enough to be fully specified, and it stalls when the domain is as wide as medicine itself.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.01, 0.05, and 0.01, which sum to 0.11, so the mean absolute error is 0.11 divided by 5, which is 0.022. The squared gaps are 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0001, 0.0025, and 0.0001, which sum to 0.0035, so the mean squared error is 0.0007 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.0007, which is 0.0265. The largest single gap is 0.05. Small errors on a worked example prove the arithmetic, not the medicine; the medicine was proved, or not, by evaluation against real cases. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind these systems is the science of differential diagnosis: a finding points to many diseases, and the task is to weigh the evidence and rank the candidates. PUFF worked because pulmonary function has a small, well-defined set of measurements and a small set of interpretations, so a few dozen rules could cover the space. CADUCEUS struggled because internal medicine has hundreds of diseases that interact, and the knowledge needed to separate them is not a list of rules but a web of causes. The science says the difficulty of a diagnostic system grows with the interactions in its domain, not with the number of diseases alone. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.88, 0.9), (0.72, 0.7), (0.91, 0.9), (0.55, 0.6), (0.79, 0.8)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYS000005:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(0.88, 0.9), (0.72, 0.7), (0.91, 0.9), (0.55, 0.6), (0.79, 0.8)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYS000005().run())

CYC: The Common-Sense Knowledge Base JAH-ESYS-000006 · 6

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

CYC is the longest-running bet in expert systems: the idea that a machine cannot reason well about anything until it knows the obvious things every person knows, like water flows downhill, a glass can break, and a person cannot be in two places at once. Started in 1984 by Doug Lenat, it set out to write down common sense as millions of assertions in a formal language, and it has been going ever since, growing to millions of assertions with a company, Cycorp, behind it. It never became the foundation of artificial intelligence its founder hoped for, but it remains the largest hand-built knowledge base ever attempted, and its history is the history of how far hand-built knowledge can go.

History

Cyc began in 1984 at the Microelectronics and Computer Technology Corporation in Austin, Texas, under Doug Lenat, and moved to the company Cycorp in 1994. The plan was to encode common sense in a formal language called CycL, starting with the most basic facts about time, space, cause, and everyday objects, and building upward. By the 2000s the knowledge base held millions of assertions, and in 2002 a portion was released as OpenCyc for public use. The system was meant to give other programs a background of ordinary knowledge so they could understand text, answer questions, and avoid absurd conclusions. It has been used in research and in some commercial applications, but it never became the universal foundation Lenat envisioned, and the field largely moved to learning from data instead of writing knowledge by hand.

Description

CYC is the longest-running bet in expert systems: the idea that a machine cannot reason well about anything until it knows the obvious things every person knows, like water flows downhill, a glass can break, and a person cannot be in two places at once. Started in 1984 by Doug Lenat, it set out to write down common sense as millions of assertions in a formal language, and it has been going ever since, growing to millions of assertions with a company, Cycorp, behind it. It never became the foundation of artificial intelligence its founder hoped for, but it remains the largest hand-built knowledge base ever attempted, and its history is the history of how far hand-built knowledge can go.

The Problem

What is the Cyc project and its attempt to encode common-sense knowledge, begun in 1984, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take a fact of common sense and write it as a formal assertion in CycL, with its terms defined in the ontology. Step 2: file the assertion in the knowledge base, linked to the concepts it mentions. Step 3: when a question arrives, use inference to combine assertions, following the links from what is asked to what is known. Step 4: report the answer with the chain of assertions behind it. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five knowledge-base sections with assertion counts of 1200, 850, 2100, 640, and 430, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a count of the real Cyc knowledge base. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"started": 1984, "opencyc_year": 2002, "assertions_millions": true, "total_worked": 5220}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the scale and the dates: begun in 1984, millions of assertions by the 2000s, OpenCyc released in 2002, and the project still running four decades later. The worked example shows the ledger arithmetic: the five section counts 1200, 850, 2100, 640, and 430 total 5220, with a mean of 1044.0, a smallest of 430, and a largest of 2100. The result that matters is the endurance: no other hand-built knowledge base has been maintained for forty years, and that endurance is itself the finding, because it shows both what hand-built knowledge can accumulate and what it costs to keep accumulating it. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 2100, mean: 1044.0, n: 5, smallest: 430, total: 5220.

When It Applies

Cyc applies wherever a stable body of background knowledge must be explicit, checkable, and auditable: controlled vocabularies, ontologies for specific domains, and systems where every conclusion must be traceable to a stated fact. It applies as a supplement to learning systems, giving them facts they cannot easily learn, and as a research instrument for studying what common sense contains. It does not apply as the foundation of general intelligence, it does not apply where the knowledge changes faster than it can be written, and it does not apply where the cost of writing the knowledge exceeds the value of having it.

Limits

The limits are the limits of hand-built knowledge at scale. Writing common sense by hand is slow: millions of assertions took decades, and the end is not in sight, because common sense has no edge. The assertions interact, so a change in one place can break reasoning somewhere else, and finding the break takes expert effort. The knowledge is also brittle in the same way every expert system is brittle: it covers what its authors wrote and nothing else. The field's verdict, delivered by the success of learning from data, is that hand-built knowledge is too slow to be the foundation of general intelligence, even if it remains useful as a supplement.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five counts 1200, 850, 2100, 640, and 430 add to 5220, and 5220 divided by 5 is 1044.0. The smallest count is 430 and the largest is 2100. These are small, transparent numbers, and their transparency is the point: a knowledge base that cannot be counted cannot be managed, and Cyc's discipline was to keep every assertion explicit and countable, even at the scale of millions. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind Cyc is the science of background knowledge: human reasoning runs on a vast store of unstated facts, and a machine without that store will misunderstand the simplest sentences. Cyc's bet was that the store could be written down explicitly, in a formal language, by a team working for decades. The science says the bet was half right: the facts can be written down, and they do help the systems that use them, but the writing is slow, the facts interact in ways that are hard to foresee, and a learned system can acquire the same background faster by reading text. Cyc remains the clearest test of the hand-built approach, and its history is the evidence for the verdict. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('time', 1200), ('space', 850), ('cause', 2100), ('objects', 640), ('events', 430)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

CLIPS: NASA's Free Rule Engine JAH-ESYS-000007 · 7

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

CLIPS is the expert-system shell that outlived the boom: a fast, free, forward-chaining rule engine built at NASA in 1985, released into the public domain, and still used decades later. It took the lessons of the research systems, the RETE algorithm for efficient pattern matching, the separation of rules from the engine, and the ability to embed the engine inside a larger program, and packaged them as a tool anyone could download and build on. Where MYCIN was a demonstration and XCON was a single application, CLIPS was infrastructure, and infrastructure is what survives.

History

CLIPS, the C Language Integrated Production System, was built at NASA's Johnson Space Center starting in 1985, led by Gary Riley and colleagues. NASA needed a rule-based tool that could be embedded in flight software and ground systems, and the existing shells were written in Lisp, expensive, or both. CLIPS was written in C, it was fast, it used the RETE algorithm for pattern matching, and NASA released it into the public domain, which made it the standard teaching and prototyping tool for a generation. It was used in NASA projects, in industry, and in universities, and it influenced the design of later rule engines including the Java-based Jess and the modern business-rules engines. Its longevity is the point: a well-built, free, embeddable engine outlasts the fashion that produced it.

Description

CLIPS is the expert-system shell that outlived the boom: a fast, free, forward-chaining rule engine built at NASA in 1985, released into the public domain, and still used decades later. It took the lessons of the research systems, the RETE algorithm for efficient pattern matching, the separation of rules from the engine, and the ability to embed the engine inside a larger program, and packaged them as a tool anyone could download and build on. Where MYCIN was a demonstration and XCON was a single application, CLIPS was infrastructure, and infrastructure is what survives.

The Problem

What is the CLIPS rule-based expert system shell, built at NASA Johnson Space Center in 1985, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: write the rules as productions, each with a left-hand side of patterns and a right-hand side of actions. Step 2: load the facts into working memory. Step 3: let the RETE network match the patterns against the facts efficiently, firing the rules whose patterns match. Step 4: run the fired rules' actions, which assert or retract facts and may trigger further rules, until no more rules fire or a halt is reached. The demo runs a small worked example of the chaining step: it takes five inference stages with rules fired of 12, 25, 18, 30, and 9, and reports the total fired, the mean per stage, and the busiest stage. The demo is a worked example of the chaining arithmetic, not a record of a CLIPS run. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"started": 1985, "total_fired_worked": 94, "mean_per_stage_worked": 18.8}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates and the design: built from 1985 at NASA Johnson Space Center, written in C, public domain, and still in use decades later. The worked example shows the chaining arithmetic: the five stages fire 12, 25, 18, 30, and 9 rules, for a total of 94 rules fired, a mean of 18.8 per stage, and a busiest stage of 30. The result that matters is the survival: CLIPS is still downloadable and still taught, which makes it the longest-lived artifact of the expert-systems era, and its survival is evidence that a clean, fast, free engine is worth more than a cleverer one that cannot be embedded. The demo prints these checked figures: max_stage: 30, mean: 18.8, stages: 5, total_fired: 94.

When It Applies

CLIPS applies wherever a fast, embeddable rule engine is needed and the rules can be written and maintained: flight and ground software, industrial control, configuration, teaching, and prototyping. It applies as infrastructure inside a larger program, not as a standalone answer, and only where a human can write and check the rules. It does not apply where the rules cannot be written, where the knowledge must be learned from data, or where the reasoning needs probabilities rather than crisp matches.

Limits

The limits are the limits of every forward-chaining shell. CLIPS gives you an engine, not knowledge: the rules still have to be written by a human, the knowledge-acquisition bottleneck is unchanged, and a large rule base is as hard to maintain in CLIPS as anywhere else. It has no learning, no uncertainty handling built in, and no common sense. Its success is a success of packaging, not of solving the hard problems, and the honest account of CLIPS says so: it made rule-based systems cheap and fast, and the hard problems stayed hard.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a chain. The five stages fire 12, 25, 18, 30, and 9 rules, which sum to 94, and 94 divided by 5 is 18.8. The busiest stage fires 30 rules and the quietest fires 9. These numbers describe one worked run, and their transparency is the point: a rule engine that cannot report how many rules fired, and where, cannot be debugged, and CLIPS's design kept that accounting visible. Formal statement: total fired = sum of rules fired per stage; mean per stage = total fired / number of stages; largest stage = max of rules fired per stage. The equation states the chaining check the demo performs: it adds the rules fired at each stage of the worked example, averages them per stage, and picks out the busiest stage.

Science Proof

The science behind CLIPS is the science of efficient pattern matching: a naive rule engine checks every rule against every fact on every cycle, which is far too slow, and the RETE algorithm avoids that by remembering partial matches in a network and updating them only when facts change. CLIPS's contribution was to make that efficiency available in C, in the public domain, and embedded in other programs, so that rule-based reasoning could live inside flight software, ground systems, and industrial controllers rather than only in a laboratory. The science says the engine matters as much as the rules: a rule base is only useful if it runs fast enough to matter. Scientific relation: An inference chain fires rules stage by stage; its size is the total number of rule firings and its shape is how those firings spread across the stages.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Skeptical analysis

The limits are the limits of every forward-chaining shell. CLIPS gives you an engine, not knowledge: the rules still have to be written by a human, the knowledge-acquisition bottleneck is unchanged, and a large rule base is as hard to maintain in CLIPS as anywhere else. It has no learning, no uncertainty handling built in, and no common sense. Its success is a success of packaging, not of solving the hard problems, and the honest account of CLIPS says so: it made rule-based systems cheap and fast, and the hard problems stayed hard.

  • dimensions: Scope: the CLIPS rule-based expert system shell, built at NASA Johnson Space Center in 1985. Worked example figures: {"started": 1985, "total_fired_worked": 94, "mean_per_stage_worked": 18.8}.

Working demo program

import hashlib
chain = [('match', 12), ('select', 25), ('fire', 18), ('update', 30), ('halt', 9)]
def compute():
    fired = [f for _, f in chain]
    total_fired = sum(fired)
    stages = len(fired)
    max_stage = max(fired)
    mean = round(total_fired / stages, 4)
    out = {"total_fired": total_fired, "stages": stages, "max_stage": max_stage, "mean": mean}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Forward Chaining: Reasoning from the Facts JAH-ESYS-000008 · 8

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Forward chaining is the inference strategy that starts with what is known and works forward: given a set of facts, find every rule whose premises match, fire those rules, add their conclusions as new facts, and repeat until nothing new follows or a goal is reached. It is the strategy of CLIPS and OPS5, of data-driven monitoring and configuration, and of every system that must notice what follows from the data rather than prove a particular claim. Its virtue is completeness within its rules: if the facts imply a conclusion, forward chaining will eventually find it, provided the rule base is complete and the process terminates.

History

Forward chaining grew out of the production-system work of the 1970s, with the OPS family at Carnegie Mellon and the RETE algorithm of 1979 making it fast enough to use. The strategy was the natural one for systems like XCON, which started with an order and had to derive a configuration, and for monitoring systems, which start with sensor readings and must derive alarms. The RETE algorithm, published by Charles Forgy in his 1979 doctoral work, made forward chaining practical by remembering partial matches instead of rechecking every rule on every cycle. CLIPS, built at NASA from 1985, made it public-domain infrastructure. The strategy's history is the history of the data-driven half of expert systems, and it remains the standard approach in business-rules engines today.

Description

Forward chaining is the inference strategy that starts with what is known and works forward: given a set of facts, find every rule whose premises match, fire those rules, add their conclusions as new facts, and repeat until nothing new follows or a goal is reached. It is the strategy of CLIPS and OPS5, of data-driven monitoring and configuration, and of every system that must notice what follows from the data rather than prove a particular claim. Its virtue is completeness within its rules: if the facts imply a conclusion, forward chaining will eventually find it, provided the rule base is complete and the process terminates.

The Problem

What is forward chaining, the data-driven inference strategy of rule-based expert systems, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: load the facts into working memory. Step 2: match the rules against the facts, using the RETE network to find every rule whose premises are satisfied. Step 3: fire the matched rules, adding their conclusions as new facts. Step 4: repeat the match-fire cycle until no new facts appear or a halt rule fires. The demo runs a small worked example of the cycle: it takes five cycles with new facts added of 14, 9, 22, 6, and 11, and reports the total new facts, the mean per cycle, and the largest cycle. The demo is a worked example of the counting arithmetic, not a record of a historical run. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"rete_year": 1979, "clips_year": 1985, "total_new_facts_worked": 62, "mean_per_cycle_worked": 12.4}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: production systems in the 1970s, the RETE algorithm in 1979, CLIPS from 1985, and the strategy still standard in business-rules engines. The worked example shows the cycle arithmetic: the five cycles add 14, 9, 22, 6, and 11 new facts, for a total of 62, a mean of 12.4 per cycle, and a largest cycle of 22. The result that matters is the guarantee: forward chaining derives every conclusion the facts and rules imply, which is exactly what a monitoring or configuration system needs, and exactly what makes it expensive when the rule base is large. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 22, mean: 12.4, n: 5, smallest: 6, total: 62.

When It Applies

Forward chaining applies wherever the facts arrive first and the conclusions must follow: monitoring, alarming, configuration, and data-driven classification. It applies where the rule base is complete enough that deriving everything is safe, and where the RETE-style efficiency keeps the cost acceptable. It does not apply where a single question should guide the search, where the rule base is so large that deriving everything is wasteful, or where the conclusions must be ranked by probability rather than derived by logic.

Limits

The limits are the limits of deriving everything. Forward chaining can fire rules that have nothing to do with the question at hand, wasting work and burying the useful conclusions in noise. It can loop, if the rules allow a conclusion to trigger itself, and it can explode, if each new fact enables many new rules. It needs a conflict-resolution strategy when several rules match at once, and the choice of strategy changes what the system does. It is the right tool when the data must drive the reasoning, and the wrong tool when a single goal should drive it.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of accumulation. The five cycles add 14, 9, 22, 6, and 11 new facts, which sum to 62, and 62 divided by 5 is 12.4. The largest cycle adds 22 facts and the smallest adds 6. These numbers describe one worked run, and they illustrate the characteristic shape of forward chaining: early cycles add many facts as the obvious conclusions fire, and later cycles add fewer as the system approaches closure. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind forward chaining is the science of deductive closure: given facts and rules, the set of derivable conclusions is fixed, and the strategy computes it by repeated application. The science says the cost is the issue, not the correctness: a naive implementation rechecks everything on every cycle, and the RETE algorithm's contribution was to make the cost proportional to what changed rather than to the size of the whole rule base. The strategy works best when the facts arrive over time and the conclusions must be kept current, which is the monitoring and configuration case, and worst when the goal is a single answer, where backward chaining is cheaper. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

import hashlib
ledger = [('cycle 1', 14), ('cycle 2', 9), ('cycle 3', 22), ('cycle 4', 6), ('cycle 5', 11)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
