Backward Chaining: Reasoning from the Goal JAH-ESYS-000009 · 9

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Backward chaining is the inference strategy that starts with the question and works backward: given a goal to prove, find the rules whose conclusions match the goal, try to prove their premises, and recurse until the premises are facts or the chain fails. It is the strategy of MYCIN, which started with the goal of choosing a therapy and worked back to the evidence, and of Prolog, which made it the foundation of logic programming. Its virtue is focus: it only explores the rules that bear on the question, and it can explain itself by showing the chain from the goal to the facts.

History

Backward chaining was the strategy of the earliest diagnostic expert systems, above all MYCIN at Stanford in the 1970s, which worked backward from the goal of recommending a therapy to the evidence about the patient. Prolog, developed in 1972 by Alain Colmerauer and Philippe Roussel at Marseille, made backward chaining the execution model of a whole programming language, and the language's success carried the strategy into teaching and research worldwide. The strategy's history is the history of the goal-driven half of expert systems, and it remains the standard approach wherever a system must answer a specific question and show its reasoning, from medical diagnosis to legal reasoning to logic programming.

Description

Backward chaining is the inference strategy that starts with the question and works backward: given a goal to prove, find the rules whose conclusions match the goal, try to prove their premises, and recurse until the premises are facts or the chain fails. It is the strategy of MYCIN, which started with the goal of choosing a therapy and worked back to the evidence, and of Prolog, which made it the foundation of logic programming. Its virtue is focus: it only explores the rules that bear on the question, and it can explain itself by showing the chain from the goal to the facts.

The Problem

What is backward chaining, the goal-driven inference strategy of rule-based expert systems, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the goal to be proved, for example that a particular organism is causing the infection. Step 2: find the rules whose conclusions match the goal. Step 3: try to prove each rule's premises, recursing into subgoals where a premise is itself a conclusion of another rule, and asking the user where a premise is a fact that must be supplied. Step 4: succeed when a chain reaches known facts, fail when every chain fails, and report the chain as the explanation. The demo runs a small worked example of the search: it takes five goal attempts with subgoals explored of 8, 15, 6, 12, and 10, and reports the total subgoals explored, the mean per attempt, and the largest attempt. The demo is a worked example of the counting arithmetic, not a record of a historical run. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"prolog_year": 1972, "total_subgoals_worked": 51, "mean_per_attempt_worked": 10.2}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Prolog in 1972, MYCIN's development from 1972 to 1980, and the strategy still standard in logic programming and diagnostic systems. The worked example shows the search arithmetic: the five attempts explore 8, 15, 6, 12, and 10 subgoals, for a total of 51, a mean of 10.2 per attempt, and a largest attempt of 15. The result that matters is the focus: backward chaining explores only what the goal requires, which is why it is cheap when the goal is narrow and expensive when the goal is broad. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 15, mean: 10.2, n: 5, smallest: 6, total: 51.

When It Applies

Backward chaining applies wherever a specific question must be answered and the reasoning must be shown: diagnosis, legal reasoning, troubleshooting, and logic programming. It applies where the user can supply facts on demand and where the goal is narrow enough that the search stays small. It does not apply where the data must drive the reasoning, where the goal is broad or open-ended, or where the same subgoal would be recomputed many times without caching.

Limits

The limits are the limits of goal-directed search. Backward chaining can loop on circular rules, it can repeat the same subgoal many times without remembering the result, and it can be slow when the goal has many matching rules. It needs facts from the user at the moment it needs them, which makes it awkward for monitoring, where the facts arrive on their own schedule. And its focus is also its blindness: it never notices a conclusion that does not bear on the goal, even when that conclusion matters. It is the right tool for answering a question, and the wrong tool for watching a stream of data.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a search tree. The five attempts explore 8, 15, 6, 12, and 10 subgoals, which sum to 51, and 51 divided by 5 is 10.2. The largest attempt explores 15 subgoals and the smallest explores 6. These numbers describe one worked run, and they illustrate the characteristic shape of backward chaining: the work is concentrated on the attempts whose goals have the most rules behind them, and the total is the sum of the branches actually explored. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind backward chaining is the science of goal-directed search: a question defines a tree of subquestions, and the strategy explores that tree depth-first, asking for facts only when it needs them. The science says the cost depends on the shape of the tree: a narrow goal with few rules is cheap, a broad goal with many rules is expensive, and a goal with circular rules can loop forever without a check. The strategy works best when the user can supply facts on demand, which is the consultation case, and worst when the facts arrive on their own, where forward chaining is the natural choice. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Step 3: try to prove each rule's premises, recursing into subgoals where a premise is itself a conclusion of another rule, and asking the user where a premise is a fact that must be supplied. Step 4: succeed when a chain reaches known facts, fail when every chain fails, and report the chain as the explanation. The demo runs a small worked example of the search: it takes five goal attempts with subgoals explored of 8, 15, 6, 12, and 10, and reports the total subgoals explored, the mean per attempt, and the largest attempt. The demo is a worked example of the counting arithmetic, not a record of a historical run.

Dataset

Dataset: backward chaining; Prolog 1972; MYCIN 1972-1980. Demo: worked example totaling subgoals explored across five goal attempts. Provenance: public-harvest from the cited public sources; licensing: public facts and figures, freely reusable, with sources cited. Last verified 2026-11-16.

Formal equation

total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts

Formal equation, in plain words

The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Formal status

Scientific equation

A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.

Proof sketch

The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

The checkable results are the dates: Prolog in 1972, MYCIN's development from 1972 to 1980, and the strategy still standard in logic programming and diagnostic systems. The worked example shows the search arithmetic: the five attempts explore 8, 15, 6, 12, and 10 subgoals, for a total of 51, a mean of 10.2 per attempt, and a largest attempt of 15. The result that matters is the focus: backward chaining explores only what the goal requires, which is why it is cheap when the goal is narrow and expensive when the goal is broad. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 15, mean: 10.2, n: 5, smallest: 6, total: 51.

Skeptical analysis

The limits are the limits of goal-directed search. Backward chaining can loop on circular rules, it can repeat the same subgoal many times without remembering the result, and it can be slow when the goal has many matching rules. It needs facts from the user at the moment it needs them, which makes it awkward for monitoring, where the facts arrive on their own schedule. And its focus is also its blindness: it never notices a conclusion that does not bear on the goal, even when that conclusion matters. It is the right tool for answering a question, and the wrong tool for watching a stream of data.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('attempt 1', 8), ('attempt 2', 15), ('attempt 3', 6), ('attempt 4', 12), ('attempt 5', 10)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

class ProgJAHESYS000009:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('attempt 1', 8), ('attempt 2', 15), ('attempt 3', 6), ('attempt 4', 12), ('attempt 5', 10)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Certainty Factors: Reasoning with Degrees of Belief JAH-ESYS-000010 · 10

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Certainty factors were MYCIN's answer to the hardest problem in expert systems: how to reason when the evidence is not certain. A certainty factor is a number from -1 to 1 that says how strongly a piece of evidence supports a conclusion, with 1 for certain belief, -1 for certain disbelief, and 0 for no evidence either way. Rules carry certainty factors, evidence carries certainty factors, and the combination rules say how they add up. The model was never a probability calculus, and its authors said so, but it worked well enough to carry MYCIN to its famous evaluation result, and it taught the field that uncertainty must be handled explicitly, not ignored.

History

Certainty factors were developed at Stanford in the 1970s for MYCIN by Edward Shortliffe, Bruce Buchanan, and their colleagues. The model came from the practical need to combine evidence: a doctor's finding might support a diagnosis strongly, weakly, or not at all, and the system needed a number for that strength. The combination rules were defined operationally: two positive factors combine as their sum minus their product, two negative factors combine symmetrically, and a positive and a negative factor combine by a different rule that lets the stronger one win. The model was influential through the 1980s and then largely replaced by Bayesian methods, which have a firmer mathematical foundation, but the lesson survived: a rule-based system that cannot say how sure it is cannot be trusted.

Description

Certainty factors were MYCIN's answer to the hardest problem in expert systems: how to reason when the evidence is not certain. A certainty factor is a number from -1 to 1 that says how strongly a piece of evidence supports a conclusion, with 1 for certain belief, -1 for certain disbelief, and 0 for no evidence either way. Rules carry certainty factors, evidence carries certainty factors, and the combination rules say how they add up. The model was never a probability calculus, and its authors said so, but it worked well enough to carry MYCIN to its famous evaluation result, and it taught the field that uncertainty must be handled explicitly, not ignored.

The Problem

What is the certainty-factor model of uncertain reasoning, developed for MYCIN in the 1970s, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: assign a certainty factor to each piece of evidence, from -1 to 1. Step 2: when a rule fires, combine the rule's certainty factor with the certainty factor of its premises to get the certainty of its conclusion. Step 3: when several rules support the same conclusion, combine their certainty factors with the combination rule, so the support accumulates. Step 4: report the conclusion with its final certainty factor. The demo runs a small worked example of the combination step: it combines three pairs of positive certainty factors, 0.6 with 0.7, 0.5 with 0.8, and 0.9 with 0.5, and reports each combined value and their mean. The demo is a worked example of the combination arithmetic, not a record of a historical consultation. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"range_low": -1, "range_high": 1, "mean_combined_worked": 0.91}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the model and its arithmetic: the combination rule for two positive factors is their sum minus their product, and the worked example shows it. The pairs 0.6 and 0.7 combine to 0.88, 0.5 and 0.8 combine to 0.9, and 0.9 and 0.5 combine to 0.95, for a mean combined certainty of 0.91. The result that matters is the shape of the rule: each new piece of evidence adds less than the last as the total approaches 1, which is the behaviour a doctor would expect from accumulating evidence, and it is the reason the model felt right to the clinicians who used it. The demo prints these checked figures: mean_combined: 0.91, n: 3.

When It Applies

Certainty factors apply as a teaching device and as a lightweight way to combine evidence where a full probability model is unavailable: simple advisory systems, teaching tools, and prototypes. They apply where the numbers are treated as scores rather than probabilities, and where the users understand that the scores are judgments, not measurements. They do not apply where the probabilities must be calibrated, where the evidence must be combined with statistical data, or where a decision depends on the number being a real probability.

Limits

The limits are the limits of an ad-hoc calculus. Certainty factors are not probabilities, so they cannot be calibrated against frequencies and cannot be combined with statistical models. They depend on the expert who assigns them, and two experts will assign different numbers to the same evidence. The combination rules were chosen to feel right rather than derived from axioms, and they can produce results that no probability calculus would endorse. The model's historical role is secure, and its replacement by Bayesian reasoning is equally secure: the field learned from certainty factors that uncertainty must be explicit, and then it learned to do it properly.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the combination rule. For 0.6 and 0.7 the sum is 1.3 and the product is 0.42, so the combined value is 0.88. For 0.5 and 0.8 the sum is 1.3 and the product is 0.4, so the combined value is 0.9. For 0.9 and 0.5 the sum is 1.4 and the product is 0.45, so the combined value is 0.95. The mean of 0.88, 0.9, and 0.95 is 2.73 divided by 3, which is 0.91. The rule keeps every result below 1, which is what stops the system from claiming certainty on the strength of weak evidence. Formal statement: CF_combined = CF1 + CF2 - CF1 * CF2, for two positive certainty factors CF1 and CF2; mean combined = (sum of combined values) / n. The equation states the certainty-factor check the demo performs: it combines each pair of positive certainty factors by the MYCIN rule and averages the combined values.

Science Proof

The science behind certainty factors is the science of pragmatic uncertainty: a system that must act on incomplete evidence needs a number for how sure it is, and the number must combine sensibly when new evidence arrives. The model was designed to satisfy intuitions rather than axioms: more evidence should increase belief, conflicting evidence should decrease it, and no amount of weak evidence should produce certainty. The science says the model satisfied those intuitions and failed the stricter test: its numbers are not probabilities, they cannot be combined with real statistical data, and different experts assign different factors to the same evidence. The field kept the intuition and replaced the arithmetic with Bayesian methods. Scientific relation: A certainty factor states how strongly evidence supports a conclusion, from -1 for certain disbelief to 1 for certain belief; two pieces of supporting evidence combine to a stronger factor than either alone.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
cfs = [(0.6, 0.7), (0.5, 0.8), (0.9, 0.5)]
def compute():
    combined = [a + b - a * b for a, b in cfs]
    mean_combined = round(sum(combined) / len(combined), 4)
    n = len(cfs)
    out = {"mean_combined": mean_combined, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

The Knowledge Acquisition Bottleneck JAH-ESYS-000011 · 11

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

The knowledge acquisition bottleneck is the reason expert systems were expensive: getting the knowledge out of an expert's head and into a rule base turned out to be the hardest part of the whole enterprise. Experts know far more than they can say, their knowledge is tied to cases and context, and the process of interviewing them, writing rules, testing the rules, and fixing the mistakes took months or years for a single system. Feigenbaum named the problem, and it named the fate of the field: systems were only as good as the knowledge their builders could extract, and extraction was slow, costly, and never finished.

History

The bottleneck was identified in the 1970s by Edward Feigenbaum and the builders of the first expert systems, who found that the programming was the easy part and the knowledge was the hard part. A knowledge engineer would interview an expert for hours, write rules from the answers, test the rules on cases, and return with the failures for another round, and the cycle repeated for months. MYCIN's roughly 600 rules, XCON's roughly 2,500 rules, and CYC's millions of assertions all represent the same cost, multiplied by scale: every rule was written, checked, and maintained by a human. The bottleneck shaped the field's economics: a system was worth building only where the expertise was scarce, valuable, and stable enough to justify the extraction cost, and the failure to solve the bottleneck is the main reason the expert-systems boom ended.

Description

The knowledge acquisition bottleneck is the reason expert systems were expensive: getting the knowledge out of an expert's head and into a rule base turned out to be the hardest part of the whole enterprise. Experts know far more than they can say, their knowledge is tied to cases and context, and the process of interviewing them, writing rules, testing the rules, and fixing the mistakes took months or years for a single system. Feigenbaum named the problem, and it named the fate of the field: systems were only as good as the knowledge their builders could extract, and extraction was slow, costly, and never finished.

The Problem

What is the knowledge acquisition bottleneck, the central difficulty of building expert systems, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: interview the expert, using cases and examples rather than abstract questions, because experts explain themselves best through cases. Step 2: write the knowledge as rules, with the expert checking each one. Step 3: test the rules on cases the expert has already solved, and record every disagreement. Step 4: return to the expert with the disagreements, refine the rules, and repeat until the system agrees with the expert on the test cases. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five acquisition rounds with rules written of 45, 60, 38, 72, and 51, and reports the total rules written, the mean per round, and the largest round. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a record of a historical acquisition effort. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mycin_rules_about": 600, "xcon_rules_about": 2500, "total_rules_worked": 266, "mean_per_round_worked": 53.2}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the scale figures that show the cost: about 600 rules for MYCIN, about 2,500 for XCON, and millions of assertions for Cyc, each written by hand over months or years. The worked example shows the ledger arithmetic: the five rounds write 45, 60, 38, 72, and 51 rules, for a total of 266, a mean of 53.2 per round, and a largest round of 72. The result that matters is the rate: at fifty rules a round, a thousand-rule system takes twenty rounds, and twenty rounds of expert interviews is a project, not a task. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 72, mean: 53.2, n: 5, smallest: 38, total: 266.

When It Applies

The bottleneck applies as a planning discipline: before building any rule-based system, count the rules, estimate the extraction cost per rule, and ask whether the expertise is stable enough to be worth extracting. It applies to every knowledge-based project, from medical diagnosis to configuration to legal reasoning, and it applies to the maintenance as much as to the building, because a rule base decays the moment the world changes. It does not apply as an excuse: a system whose knowledge cannot be extracted should not be built as a rule-based system at all, and the honest answer is to learn from data instead.

Limits

The limits are the limits of the approach itself. The bottleneck cannot be removed by better interviewing, because the knowledge that matters most is the knowledge the expert cannot state. It cannot be removed by more knowledge engineers, because the expert's time is the binding constraint. It can only be bypassed, by learning from data instead of asking for rules, and that bypass is the history of the field after the 1980s. The bottleneck remains the standard against which every knowledge-based proposal must be measured: who will write the knowledge, how long will it take, and who will maintain it when the world changes.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five rounds write 45, 60, 38, 72, and 51 rules, which sum to 266, and 266 divided by 5 is 53.2. The largest round writes 72 rules and the smallest writes 38. These numbers describe one worked example, and they make the bottleneck concrete: the cost of a rule base is the number of rules times the cost of extracting each one, and the extraction cost is measured in expert hours, which are the scarcest hours there are. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind the bottleneck is the science of tacit knowledge: experts know how to do things they cannot describe, because their knowledge is compiled into pattern recognition and habit. Interviewing extracts the describable part and misses the rest, and the missing part shows up as failures on cases the expert would have handled easily. The science says the bottleneck is not a failure of technique but a fact about expertise: knowledge that took a career to build cannot be transferred in an interview, and any system built by transfer will be thinner than the expert it imitates. Machine learning later attacked the bottleneck from the other side, learning from cases instead of asking for rules. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Sources

  • Knowledge acquisition — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge_acquisition (Defines knowledge acquisition and the bottleneck problem.)
  • Knowledge engineering — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge_engineering (Defines knowledge engineering and the knowledge engineer role.)
  • Expert system — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expert_system (Defines expert systems, their rule bases, inference engines, and history.)

History

The bottleneck was identified in the 1970s by Edward Feigenbaum and the builders of the first expert systems, who found that the programming was the easy part and the knowledge was the hard part. A knowledge engineer would interview an expert for hours, write rules from the answers, test the rules on cases, and return with the failures for another round, and the cycle repeated for months. MYCIN's roughly 600 rules, XCON's roughly 2,500 rules, and CYC's millions of assertions all represent the same cost, multiplied by scale: every rule was written, checked, and maintained by a human. The bottleneck shaped the field's economics: a system was worth building only where the expertise was scarce, valuable, and stable enough to justify the extraction cost, and the failure to solve the bottleneck is the main reason the expert-systems boom ended.

Problem

What is the knowledge acquisition bottleneck, the central difficulty of building expert systems, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

Method

Step 1: interview the expert, using cases and examples rather than abstract questions, because experts explain themselves best through cases. Step 2: write the knowledge as rules, with the expert checking each one. Step 3: test the rules on cases the expert has already solved, and record every disagreement. Step 4: return to the expert with the disagreements, refine the rules, and repeat until the system agrees with the expert on the test cases. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five acquisition rounds with rules written of 45, 60, 38, 72, and 51, and reports the total rules written, the mean per round, and the largest round. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a record of a historical acquisition effort.

Dataset

Dataset: knowledge acquisition bottleneck, identified 1970s; MYCIN about 600 rules; XCON about 2,500 rules; Cyc millions of assertions. Demo: worked example totaling rules written across five acquisition rounds. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Proof sketch

Proof status

Results reported

The checkable results are the scale figures that show the cost: about 600 rules for MYCIN, about 2,500 for XCON, and millions of assertions for Cyc, each written by hand over months or years. The worked example shows the ledger arithmetic: the five rounds write 45, 60, 38, 72, and 51 rules, for a total of 266, a mean of 53.2 per round, and a largest round of 72. The result that matters is the rate: at fifty rules a round, a thousand-rule system takes twenty rounds, and twenty rounds of expert interviews is a project, not a task. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 72, mean: 53.2, n: 5, smallest: 38, total: 266.

Skeptical analysis

The limits are the limits of the approach itself. The bottleneck cannot be removed by better interviewing, because the knowledge that matters most is the knowledge the expert cannot state. It cannot be removed by more knowledge engineers, because the expert's time is the binding constraint. It can only be bypassed, by learning from data instead of asking for rules, and that bypass is the history of the field after the 1980s. The bottleneck remains the standard against which every knowledge-based proposal must be measured: who will write the knowledge, how long will it take, and who will maintain it when the world changes.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('round 1', 45), ('round 2', 60), ('round 3', 38), ('round 4', 72), ('round 5', 51)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('round 1', 45), ('round 2', 60), ('round 3', 38), ('round 4', 72), ('round 5', 51)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

The AI Winter and the Expert-Systems Boom JAH-ESYS-000012 · 12

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

The expert-systems boom was the first great commercial wave of artificial intelligence, and the AI winter was its collapse. In the early 1980s, expert systems were the technology every large company wanted: XCON was saving Digital Equipment Corporation millions, startups sold shells and consulting, and the Lisp machine companies, Symbolics and Lisp Machines Inc., sold the hardware to run it all on. By the end of the decade the market had collapsed, the hardware companies were gone, and artificial intelligence became a phrase companies avoided. The boom and the bust are one story, and the story is about what happens when a real technology is oversold.

History

The boom began with the success of XCON and the publicity around MYCIN and DENDRAL, and it ran through the first half of the 1980s. Companies bought expert-system shells, hired knowledge engineers, and built systems for diagnosis, configuration, and planning. The Lisp machine market grew with it: Symbolics, founded in 1980, and Lisp Machines Inc. sold specialized workstations for running Lisp-based AI, and their sales tracked the expert-systems market. The collapse came in the late 1980s, when the maintenance cost of rule bases became clear, when cheaper general-purpose workstations ran Lisp well enough, and when the systems that had been promised did not arrive on schedule. The Lisp machine market crashed in 1987, Symbolics and its rivals faded, DARPA cut its AI funding, and the field entered the AI winter, a period of reduced funding and low expectations that lasted into the 1990s. The technology did not disappear: it survived in business-rules engines, in embedded systems, and in CLIPS, but the boom was over.

Description

The expert-systems boom was the first great commercial wave of artificial intelligence, and the AI winter was its collapse. In the early 1980s, expert systems were the technology every large company wanted: XCON was saving Digital Equipment Corporation millions, startups sold shells and consulting, and the Lisp machine companies, Symbolics and Lisp Machines Inc., sold the hardware to run it all on. By the end of the decade the market had collapsed, the hardware companies were gone, and artificial intelligence became a phrase companies avoided. The boom and the bust are one story, and the story is about what happens when a real technology is oversold.

The Problem

What is the rise and fall of expert systems in the 1980s and the AI winter that followed, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the boom years as a series, with the expert-systems market growing through the early 1980s. Step 2: take the bust as the second half of the series, with the market shrinking after the late 1980s. Step 3: measure the rise and the fall from the series, and note where the peak sits. Step 4: report the shape of the wave and the lesson it carries. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five index values for the market, 20 in 1980, 45 in 1982, 80 in 1984, 100 in 1986, and 55 in 1990, and reports the earliest, the latest, the multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the mean. The demo is a worked example of the growth arithmetic on an illustrative index, not a record of historical market data. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"crash_year": 1987, "index_first": 20, "index_last": 55, "multiple_worked": 2.75, "cagr_worked": 0.1065}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: the boom through the early 1980s, the Lisp machine market crash in 1987, and the AI winter through the early 1990s. The worked example shows the growth arithmetic on the illustrative index: the earliest value is 20, the latest is 55, the multiple is 2.75, the compound annual growth rate over the ten-year span is 0.1065, and the mean is 60.0. The result that matters is the shape: a rise, a peak, and a fall, with the fall driven by maintenance costs and oversold promises rather than by any failure of the underlying idea. The demo prints these checked figures: cagr: 0.1065, earliest: 20, latest: 55, mean: 60.0, multiple: 2.75, n: 5.

When It Applies

The boom and the winter apply as a caution for every AI wave since: a real capability, oversold, will produce a winter, and the winter will punish the overselling, not the capability. They apply to planning: promise what the system can do, count the maintenance cost, and do not sell a narrow system as a general one. They do not apply as a prediction that every wave ends in winter, because some waves survive on their merits, and they do not apply as an excuse to dismiss a technology that has already proved its value in a narrow domain.

Limits

The limits are the limits of the historical account. The market figures of the 1980s are estimates, not measurements, and different accounts give different numbers, so the record states the shape and the dates rather than a precise market size. The winter's causes are also debated: maintenance costs, hardware collapse, funding cuts, and overselling all played a part, and no single cause explains the fall. The honest account says the boom was real, the bust was real, and the technology survived both, which is more useful than any single number.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of growth over a span. The illustrative index runs from 20 in 1980 to 55 in 1990, so the multiple is 55 divided by 20, which is 2.75. The compound annual growth rate is the tenth root of 2.75 minus 1, which is 0.1066. The mean of 20, 45, 80, 100, and 55 is 300 divided by 5, which is 60.0. These numbers describe an illustrative index, and their purpose is to show the method: a wave is measured by its rise, its peak, and its fall, and the measurement is the same whether the index is illustrative or historical. Formal statement: CAGR = (V_latest / V_earliest)^(1 / (t_latest - t_earliest)) - 1, with multiple = V_latest / V_earliest and mean = (sum of values) / n. The equation states the growth check the demo performs: it takes the stated series of dated values, divides the latest by the earliest for the multiple, and takes the root over the year span for the compound annual growth rate.

Science Proof

The science behind the boom and bust is the science of technology adoption: a real capability attracts investment, the investment attracts promises, the promises outrun the capability, and the gap between promise and delivery destroys the market. Expert systems were real: XCON saved money, MYCIN reasoned well, CLIPS ran fast. But the promises were general: the systems were sold as if they could capture any expertise, and they could not. The science says the winter was not a verdict on the technology but a verdict on the selling, and the proof is that the technology survived the winter in quieter forms, in business rules and embedded engines, while the hype did not. Scientific relation: A time series is a sequence of measurements ordered in time; its growth over a span is summarised by the multiple and the compound annual growth rate.. Working demo program

import hashlib
series = [(1980, 20), (1982, 45), (1984, 80), (1986, 100), (1990, 55)]
def compute():
    years = [y for y, v in series]
    values = [v for y, v in series]
    earliest = values[0]
    latest = values[-1]
    multiple = round(latest / earliest, 4)
    span = years[-1] - years[0]
    cagr = round((latest / earliest) ** (1 / span) - 1, 4)
    mean = round(sum(values) / len(values), 2)
    n = len(values)
    out = {"earliest": earliest, "latest": latest, "multiple": multiple, "cagr": cagr, "mean": mean, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Expert Systems in Medicine: From MYCIN to Clinical Decision Support JAH-ESYS-000013 · 13

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Medicine was the first home of expert systems and the field where their promise and their limits showed most clearly. MYCIN reasoned about antibiotics, PUFF interpreted lung tests in routine use, CADUCEUS tried to cover internal medicine, and DXplain at Massachusetts General Hospital gave clinicians a diagnostic reference through the 1980s. None of them became a doctor, but all of them taught the same lesson: a system that advises a clinician, shows its reasoning, and stays inside a narrow domain can help, while a system that claims the whole of medicine cannot. Modern clinical decision support descends directly from them, in drug-interaction alerts, dosing calculators, and guideline reminders.

History

The medical line runs from MYCIN at Stanford in the 1970s through PUFF's routine use at the Pacific Medical Center from 1979, INTERNIST-I and CADUCEUS at Pittsburgh through the 1970s and 1980s, and DXplain, built at Massachusetts General Hospital and released in the 1980s as a diagnostic decision-support tool. The 1980s also brought commercial medical systems and the first regulatory attention to software that advises on treatment. By the 1990s the standalone diagnostic expert system had given way to decision support embedded in the electronic health record: alerts for drug interactions, reminders for preventive care, and dosing support, all of them narrower, quieter, and more useful than their ambitious ancestors. The lineage is direct, and the narrowing is the lesson.

Description

Medicine was the first home of expert systems and the field where their promise and their limits showed most clearly. MYCIN reasoned about antibiotics, PUFF interpreted lung tests in routine use, CADUCEUS tried to cover internal medicine, and DXplain at Massachusetts General Hospital gave clinicians a diagnostic reference through the 1980s. None of them became a doctor, but all of them taught the same lesson: a system that advises a clinician, shows its reasoning, and stays inside a narrow domain can help, while a system that claims the whole of medicine cannot. Modern clinical decision support descends directly from them, in drug-interaction alerts, dosing calculators, and guideline reminders.

The Problem

What is expert systems in medicine, from MYCIN and PUFF to modern clinical decision support, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the patient's findings as input, symptoms, signs, and test results. Step 2: match the findings against the disease knowledge base and score each candidate diagnosis. Step 3: rank the candidates, report the leading diagnosis with its supporting findings, and list the alternatives. Step 4: let the clinician accept, reject, or investigate further, with the system recording the decision. The demo runs a small worked example of the ranking step: it takes five candidate diagnoses with support scores of 0.9, 0.65, 0.8, 0.5, and 0.72, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a record of a historical consultation. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"puff_year": 1979, "mean_support_worked": 0.714, "best_support_worked": 0.9}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the deployment dates: MYCIN in the 1970s, PUFF in routine use from 1979, INTERNIST-I and CADUCEUS as research systems through the 1980s, and DXplain released in the 1980s. The worked example shows the ranking arithmetic: the five support scores 0.9, 0.65, 0.8, 0.5, and 0.72 total 3.57, with a mean of 0.714, a smallest of 0.5, and a largest of 0.9. The result that matters is the shift: the systems that survived were the narrow ones embedded in clinical workflow, and the broad ones stayed in the laboratory, which is the shape of medical AI ever since. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 0.9, mean: 0.714, n: 5, smallest: 0.5, total: 3.57.

When It Applies

Medical expert systems apply as advisers inside clinical workflow: drug-interaction alerts, dosing support, guideline reminders, and diagnostic references that show their reasoning. They apply where the domain is narrow, the knowledge is maintained, and a clinician checks every recommendation. They do not apply as autonomous diagnosticians, they do not apply where the findings cannot be entered reliably, and they do not apply where a wrong ranking would be acted on without human review.

Limits

The limits are the limits of advice without responsibility. A medical expert system cannot examine a patient, cannot notice what was never entered, and cannot take responsibility for a decision, so it must always be checked by a clinician. Its knowledge freezes when its authors stop maintaining it, and medicine changes constantly. Its evaluation on paper cases does not prove it helps real patients, and the history of the field shows that adoption depends on workflow, liability, and trust as much as on accuracy. The honest account keeps all of that in view.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five support scores 0.9, 0.65, 0.8, 0.5, and 0.72 add to 3.57, and 3.57 divided by 5 is 0.714. The smallest score is 0.5 and the largest is 0.9. Ranking by score is transparent and auditable, which is why the approach suited medicine: a clinician can see the ranking, question it, and override it, and the numbers are small enough to be understood at a glance. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind medical expert systems is the science of differential diagnosis under uncertainty: findings point to many diseases, and the task is to weigh them honestly and rank the candidates. The systems worked when the domain was narrow enough for the knowledge to be complete, as in pulmonary function, and struggled when the domain was as wide as internal medicine, where diseases interact and findings overlap. The science says the difficulty grows with the interactions, not with the count of diseases, and that an advisory system must be judged by whether clinicians use it, not by whether it can pass a paper test. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('diagnosis 1', 0.9), ('diagnosis 2', 0.65), ('diagnosis 3', 0.8), ('diagnosis 4', 0.5), ('diagnosis 5', 0.72)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYS000013:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('diagnosis 1', 0.9), ('diagnosis 2', 0.65), ('diagnosis 3', 0.8), ('diagnosis 4', 0.5), ('diagnosis 5', 0.72)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYS000013().run())

Expert Systems in Law: TAXMAN and Legal Reasoning JAH-ESYS-000014 · 14

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Law was an early target for expert systems because so much of it looks like rules: statutes state conditions, cases apply them, and a conclusion follows. TAXMAN, built by Thorne McCarty at Harvard in 1977, modeled corporate tax law, representing the tax code and reasoning about whether a corporate reorganization qualified for tax-free treatment. Later systems in Britain and the Netherlands advised on benefits, housing, and immigration. None of them became a lawyer, but they showed that legal reasoning could be modeled far enough to be useful, and they exposed the exact point where rules run out: the open-textured concepts, like reasonableness, that no rule can settle.

History

TAXMAN was built in 1977 by Thorne McCarty, then at Harvard, as a research system for corporate reorganization under the United States tax code. It represented the statute as rules and the leading cases as examples, and it reasoned by analogy from cases as well as by deduction from rules, which made it more sophisticated than a simple rule base. Through the 1980s, legal expert systems spread in Britain, the Netherlands, and Australia, advising citizens on welfare benefits and housing rights and helping lawyers with document assembly. The field learned that statutes are only part of law: cases, principles, and judgment fill the gaps, and a system that captures the statutes but not the judgment will be right on the easy cases and silent on the hard ones. Modern legal technology descends from this work in document automation, compliance checking, and legal research, all of them narrower and more practical than the early ambitions.

Description

Law was an early target for expert systems because so much of it looks like rules: statutes state conditions, cases apply them, and a conclusion follows. TAXMAN, built by Thorne McCarty at Harvard in 1977, modeled corporate tax law, representing the tax code and reasoning about whether a corporate reorganization qualified for tax-free treatment. Later systems in Britain and the Netherlands advised on benefits, housing, and immigration. None of them became a lawyer, but they showed that legal reasoning could be modeled far enough to be useful, and they exposed the exact point where rules run out: the open-textured concepts, like reasonableness, that no rule can settle.

The Problem

What is expert systems in law, from TAXMAN in the 1970s to modern legal reasoning tools, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the facts of the case as input, the transaction, the parties, and the amounts. Step 2: match the facts against the statute's rules, checking each condition in turn. Step 3: where a rule's terms are open-textured, compare the case with leading cases and reason by analogy. Step 4: report the conclusion, the rules applied, and the cases relied on. The demo runs a small worked example of the checking step: it takes five statutory conditions with satisfaction scores of 1, 1, 0, 1, and 1, and reports how many are satisfied and the mean satisfaction. The demo is a worked example of the counting arithmetic, not a record of a historical legal opinion. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"taxman_year": 1977, "conditions_satisfied_worked": 4, "mean_satisfaction_worked": 0.8}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: TAXMAN in 1977, the spread of legal expert systems through the 1980s, and the modern descendants in document automation and compliance. The worked example shows the checking arithmetic: of the five conditions, 4 are satisfied and 1 is not, for a mean satisfaction of 0.8. The result that matters is the mixed verdict: the rules handle the clear cases, the analogy handles the near cases, and the hard cases still need a lawyer, which is the honest boundary of the technology. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 1, mean: 0.8, n: 5, smallest: 0, total: 4.

When It Applies

Legal expert systems apply as drafting and checking aids: document assembly, compliance checking, benefits advice, and research tools that show the rules and cases behind every conclusion. They apply where the statute is precise, the cases are settled, and a lawyer checks the result. They do not apply as autonomous legal advisers, they do not apply where the concepts are open-textured and the judgment is the whole job, and they do not apply where a wrong conclusion would harm someone who cannot check it.

Limits

The limits are the limits of law itself. Statutes are not complete, cases conflict, and open-textured concepts like reasonableness cannot be reduced to rules. A legal expert system cannot argue, cannot weigh competing principles, and cannot take responsibility for advice that harms a client. Its knowledge freezes when the law changes, and the law changes constantly. The honest account treats legal expert systems as research instruments and drafting aids, not as substitutes for lawyers, and judges them by whether they help a lawyer work, not by whether they can pass as one.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of counting. Five conditions with satisfaction values of 1, 1, 0, 1, and 1 give a total of 4 satisfied out of 5, and 4 divided by 5 is 0.8. The smallest value is 0 and the largest is 1. These are small, transparent numbers, and their transparency is the point: a legal conclusion should be traceable to the conditions that were checked and the result of each check, so that a lawyer can verify every step. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind legal expert systems is the science of rule-based reasoning about texts: statutes are written as rules, but their terms are interpreted through cases, and a system must handle both. TAXMAN's contribution was to model that duality, using rules for the statute and cases for the interpretation, and the science says that duality is essential: a purely rule-based legal system will be brittle exactly where the law is most interesting. The system worked where the statute was precise and the cases were settled, and it struggled where the concepts were open-textured, which is where legal judgment lives. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. import hashlib
ledger = [('condition 1', 1), ('condition 2', 1), ('condition 3', 0), ('condition 4', 1), ('condition 5', 1)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYS000014:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('condition 1', 1), ('condition 2', 1), ('condition 3', 0), ('condition 4', 1), ('condition 5', 1)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYS000014().run())

Expert Systems in Finance: Credit, Fraud, and Underwriting JAH-ESYS-000015 · 15

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Finance adopted expert systems early and quietly, because its decisions already looked like rules: a credit application is approved or denied by conditions, a transaction is flagged by patterns, and an insurance policy is underwritten by criteria. Through the 1980s and 1990s, banks and insurers built rule-based systems for credit scoring, fraud detection, and underwriting, and many of those systems ran for decades. The FICO credit score, introduced in 1989, showed the neighboring idea at scale: a statistical score that lenders could apply uniformly. The rule-based systems and the scores grew up together, and modern fraud detection still runs on rules alongside the statistical models that followed.

History

The financial line runs from the credit-scoring work of the 1950s, through the rule-based underwriting and fraud systems of the 1980s, to the FICO score's introduction in 1989, which standardized consumer credit scoring in the United States. Banks used rules to encode lending policy, insurers used rules to encode underwriting criteria, and card networks used rules to flag transactions that matched fraud patterns. The systems worked because financial policy is written down: a lending policy is a document, an underwriting manual is a document, and a rule base is a document made executable. The 1990s and 2000s added statistical models, and the modern stack runs both: rules for the policy that must be explained, models for the patterns that must be learned, and humans for the cases neither handles.

Description

Finance adopted expert systems early and quietly, because its decisions already looked like rules: a credit application is approved or denied by conditions, a transaction is flagged by patterns, and an insurance policy is underwritten by criteria. Through the 1980s and 1990s, banks and insurers built rule-based systems for credit scoring, fraud detection, and underwriting, and many of those systems ran for decades. The FICO credit score, introduced in 1989, showed the neighboring idea at scale: a statistical score that lenders could apply uniformly. The rule-based systems and the scores grew up together, and modern fraud detection still runs on rules alongside the statistical models that followed.

The Problem

What is expert systems in finance, from credit scoring rules to fraud detection and underwriting, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the application or transaction as input, with its facts and amounts. Step 2: match the facts against the policy rules, checking each condition in turn. Step 3: score the result, flagging the application for review when a rule fires or a threshold is crossed. Step 4: report the decision, the rules that fired, and the reasons, so the decision can be explained and appealed. The demo runs a small worked example of the scoring step: it takes five applications with policy scores of 0.82, 0.61, 0.9, 0.55, and 0.77 against reference scores of 0.8, 0.6, 0.9, 0.6, and 0.75, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the scoring arithmetic, not a record of historical lending decisions. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"fico_year": 1989, "mae_worked": 0.02, "rmse_worked": 0.0261, "max_gap_worked": 0.05}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: credit scoring from the 1950s, rule-based financial systems through the 1980s and 1990s, and the FICO score introduced in 1989. The worked example shows the scoring arithmetic: the gaps between the policy scores and their references are 0.02, 0.01, 0.0, 0.05, and 0.02, giving a mean absolute error of 0.02 and a root mean square error of 0.0265, with a largest gap of 0.05. The result that matters is the durability: financial rules are still running, because a lending policy must be explainable, and rules explain themselves. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.02, max_err: 0.05, n: 5, rmse: 0.0261.

When It Applies

Financial expert systems apply wherever a written policy must be applied consistently and explained: credit decisions, underwriting, fraud flagging, and compliance checks. They apply where the policy is stable enough to encode, where every decision must be explainable, and where a human reviews the flagged cases. They do not apply where the pattern must be learned from data rather than written as policy, where the policy changes faster than the rules can be revised, or where the rules would reproduce a bias nobody has audited.

Limits

The limits are the limits of policy written as rules. A rule base encodes the policy of the moment, and it will deny the applicant the policy would have approved after the policy changes. Rules cannot detect a fraud pattern nobody has described, which is why statistical models were added alongside them. A rule-based decision can also be unfair in a way that is hard to see: if the policy's conditions correlate with a protected characteristic, the rules will reproduce the bias faithfully and at scale. The honest account audits financial rules for bias as carefully as it audits them for accuracy.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.02, 0.01, 0.0, 0.05, and 0.02, which sum to 0.1, so the mean absolute error is 0.1 divided by 5, which is 0.02. The squared gaps are 0.0004, 0.0001, 0.0, 0.0025, and 0.0004, which sum to 0.0034, so the mean squared error is 0.00068 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.00068, which is 0.0261. The largest single gap is 0.05. Small errors on a worked example prove the arithmetic, not the lending policy; the policy was proved, or not, by its outcomes. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind financial expert systems is the science of policy as code: a lending policy, an underwriting manual, and a fraud pattern are all statements of the form if this, then that, and a rule engine executes them consistently at scale. The systems worked because the policy was written down before the system was built, so the knowledge acquisition was a transcription rather than an extraction. The science says the difficulty is not the rules but the maintenance: policy changes constantly, fraud patterns evolve, and a rule base that is not updated becomes a record of last year's policy applied to this year's cases. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.82, 0.8), (0.61, 0.6), (0.9, 0.9), (0.55, 0.6), (0.77, 0.75)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Expert Systems in Industry: Fault Diagnosis and Process Control JAH-ESYS-000016 · 16

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Industry was the quiet success story of expert systems: while the headlines went to medicine and the boom went to shells, factories and utilities used rule-based systems to diagnose faults, control processes, and configure equipment, and many of those systems ran for years without fanfare. XCON configured computers, General Electric's DELTA diagnosed diesel locomotives in the 1980s, and process plants used rules to watch sensor readings and advise operators. The industrial case suited the technology: the equipment was known, the failure modes were documented, and the rules could be written from the maintenance manual.

History

The industrial line runs from XCON at DEC in 1978 through the fault-diagnosis systems of the 1980s, including DELTA at General Electric for diesel-electric locomotive troubleshooting, and into the process-control and monitoring systems of the 1980s and 1990s. The pattern was the same everywhere: take the maintenance manual and the operators' experience, write them as rules, and run the rules against live sensor data. The systems worked because industrial knowledge is documented: a locomotive's troubleshooting guide is a book, a plant's operating procedure is a book, and a rule base is a book made executable. The 1990s added statistical monitoring alongside the rules, and modern predictive maintenance descends from both lines, the rules for the known failures and the models for the patterns nobody wrote down.

Description

Industry was the quiet success story of expert systems: while the headlines went to medicine and the boom went to shells, factories and utilities used rule-based systems to diagnose faults, control processes, and configure equipment, and many of those systems ran for years without fanfare. XCON configured computers, General Electric's DELTA diagnosed diesel locomotives in the 1980s, and process plants used rules to watch sensor readings and advise operators. The industrial case suited the technology: the equipment was known, the failure modes were documented, and the rules could be written from the maintenance manual.

The Problem

What is expert systems in industry, for fault diagnosis, process control, and configuration, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the sensor readings and operator reports as input. Step 2: match the readings against the fault rules, checking each symptom pattern in turn. Step 3: rank the candidate faults by how many symptoms match and how strongly. Step 4: report the leading fault, the evidence for it, and the recommended action. The demo runs a small worked example of the ranking step: it takes five candidate faults with match counts of 9, 14, 7, 12, and 10, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the counting arithmetic, not a record of a historical diagnosis. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"xcon_year": 1978, "total_matches_worked": 52, "mean_matches_worked": 10.4}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the deployments: XCON from 1978, DELTA in the 1980s, and process-monitoring systems through the 1980s and 1990s. The worked example shows the ranking arithmetic: the five match counts 9, 14, 7, 12, and 10 total 52, with a mean of 10.4, a smallest of 7, and a largest of 14. The result that matters is the durability: industrial rule-based systems ran for years because their knowledge came from manuals that were already written, which is the one case where the knowledge-acquisition bottleneck was never a problem. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 14, mean: 10.4, n: 5, smallest: 7, total: 52.

When It Applies

Industrial expert systems apply wherever the equipment is documented, the failure modes are known, and the operator checks the recommendation: fault diagnosis, process monitoring, configuration, and maintenance advice. They apply where the manuals already exist, where the sensor data is reliable, and where the cost of a wrong recommendation is bounded. They do not apply where the failures are novel, where the equipment changes faster than the rules, or where a sensor fault would be trusted without question.

Limits

The limits are the limits of documented knowledge. A rule base diagnoses the faults in the manual and nothing else, so a novel failure is invisible until the manual is updated. The rules must be maintained as the equipment changes, and a plant that is modified faster than its rules is a plant whose diagnostic system is out of date. Sensor faults can also mislead the system, because a rule that trusts a broken sensor will reach a wrong conclusion confidently. The honest account pairs rules with statistical monitoring and keeps a human operator in the loop.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five match counts 9, 14, 7, 12, and 10 add to 52, and 52 divided by 5 is 10.4. The smallest count is 7 and the largest is 14. Ranking by count is transparent and auditable, which is why the approach suited industry: an operator can see the ranking, check the symptoms, and override the recommendation, and the numbers are small enough to be understood at a glance. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind industrial expert systems is the science of diagnosis from symptoms: a fault produces a pattern of readings, and the task is to match the pattern to the fault. The systems worked because the patterns were documented in troubleshooting guides, the sensor data was reliable, and the cost of a wrong diagnosis was bounded by the operator checking the recommendation. The science says the difficulty is the unknown fault: a rule base diagnoses the failures its authors knew about, and a new failure mode is invisible until someone writes a rule for it, which is why statistical monitoring was added alongside. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

The rules must be maintained as the equipment changes, and a plant that is modified faster than its rules is a plant whose diagnostic system is out of date. Sensor faults can also mislead the system, because a rule that trusts a broken sensor will reach a wrong conclusion confidently. The honest account pairs rules with statistical monitoring and keeps a human operator in the loop.

Specification

  • dimensions: Scope: expert systems in industry, for fault diagnosis, process control, and configuration. Worked example figures: {"xcon_year": 1978, "total_matches_worked": 52, "mean_matches_worked": 10.4}.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('fault 1', 9), ('fault 2', 14), ('fault 3', 7), ('fault 4', 12), ('fault 5', 10)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
