Rule-Based Systems versus Machine Learning JAH-ESYS-000017 · 17

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

The comparison between rule-based systems and machine learning is the central argument in the history of artificial intelligence, and it has a clear shape: rules are transparent, auditable, and written by humans, while learning systems are opaque, data-hungry, and built from examples. Rules win where the knowledge is already written down and must be explained; learning wins where the knowledge cannot be written down and must be found in data. The field spent the 1980s on rules, the 2010s on learning, and the honest current position is that the two belong together, with rules for the policy that must be explained and learning for the patterns that must be discovered.

History

The argument runs through the whole history: DENDRAL and MYCIN in the 1970s proved rules could work, the expert-systems boom of the 1980s sold them, the AI winter of the late 1980s punished the overselling, and the machine-learning revival of the 2010s, driven by deep learning, showed what learning from data could do at scale. The comparison became practical in the 1990s and 2000s, when fraud detection, credit scoring, and medical systems began running both: rules for the policy and the known patterns, models for the unknown patterns. The hybrid position is now the standard one in industry: a learned model proposes, a rule base constrains, and a human decides, with each part doing what it is good at.

Description

The comparison between rule-based systems and machine learning is the central argument in the history of artificial intelligence, and it has a clear shape: rules are transparent, auditable, and written by humans, while learning systems are opaque, data-hungry, and built from examples. Rules win where the knowledge is already written down and must be explained; learning wins where the knowledge cannot be written down and must be found in data. The field spent the 1980s on rules, the 2010s on learning, and the honest current position is that the two belong together, with rules for the policy that must be explained and learning for the patterns that must be discovered.

The Problem

What is the comparison between rule-based expert systems and machine learning, and the hybrid that followed, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take a task and ask whether its knowledge is written down or must be learned. Step 2: where the knowledge is written down, encode it as rules and check the rules against cases. Step 3: where the knowledge must be learned, train a model on labeled examples and evaluate it on held-out data. Step 4: combine the two, using rules to constrain the model and the model to cover what the rules miss. The demo runs a small worked example of the comparison step: it takes five tasks with rule-based accuracy of 0.88, 0.72, 0.95, 0.6, and 0.81 against learned-model accuracy of 0.9, 0.75, 0.93, 0.7, and 0.85, and reports the mean absolute gap and the root mean square gap. The demo is a worked example of the comparison arithmetic, not a record of historical evaluations. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_gap_worked": 0.042, "rmse_gap_worked": 0.0516, "max_gap_worked": 0.1}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: rules in the 1970s and 1980s, the winter in the late 1980s, and the learning revival in the 2010s. The worked example shows the comparison arithmetic: the gaps between the rule-based and learned accuracies are 0.02, 0.03, 0.02, 0.1, and 0.04, giving a mean absolute gap of 0.042 and a root mean square gap of 0.051, with a largest gap of 0.1. The result that matters is the pattern: neither side wins everywhere, the learned model wins where the pattern is in the data, the rules win where the policy must be explained, and the honest comparison reports both. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.042, max_err: 0.1, n: 5, rmse: 0.0516.

When It Applies

The comparison applies as a design discipline: for every task, ask whether the knowledge is written down or in the data, whether the decision must be explained, and whether the domain changes faster than rules can be maintained. It applies to fraud, credit, medicine, and configuration, wherever both approaches are candidates. It does not apply as a slogan, it does not apply as a verdict on a whole field, and it does not apply where the task has not been named.

Limits

The limits are the limits of any comparison made in the abstract. Rules and learning are not rivals in general but tools for different cases, and a comparison that declares a winner without naming the task is meaningless. Rules fail where the knowledge cannot be written, learning fails where the data is biased or the decision must be explained, and both fail where the evaluation is dishonest. The honest account compares them on a named task, with named data, and reports where each one wins.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.02, 0.03, 0.02, 0.1, and 0.04, which sum to 0.21, so the mean absolute gap is 0.21 divided by 5, which is 0.042. The squared gaps are 0.0004, 0.0009, 0.0004, 0.01, and 0.0016, which sum to 0.0133, so the mean squared gap is 0.00266 and the root mean square gap is the square root of 0.00266, which is 0.0516. The largest single gap is 0.1. Small gaps on a worked example prove the arithmetic, not the verdict; the verdict comes from evaluating both approaches on the same task with the same data. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind the comparison is the science of knowledge representation: rules represent knowledge explicitly, in a form a human can read, check, and change, while learned models represent knowledge implicitly, in weights that no human can read. The science says the explicit form is auditable and the implicit form is powerful, and the choice between them is a choice about what matters more for the task: auditability or performance on patterns nobody wrote down. The hybrid science is the science of combining them: constrain the model with rules where the constraints are known, and let the model range where they are not. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.88, 0.9), (0.72, 0.75), (0.95, 0.93), (0.6, 0.7), (0.81, 0.85)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

The Inference Engine and the RETE Algorithm JAH-ESYS-000018 · 18

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

The inference engine is the part of an expert system that does the reasoning: it takes the rules and the facts, finds the matches, fires the rules, and keeps going until the job is done. The hard problem was speed, because a naive engine checks every rule against every fact on every cycle, and that is far too slow for a rule base of thousands. The RETE algorithm, published by Charles Forgy in 1979, solved the problem by remembering partial matches in a network and updating them only when the facts change, so the cost of a cycle depends on what changed rather than on the size of the whole rule base. It is the reason rule engines could run at all, and it is still the core of modern business-rules engines.

History

The inference engine's history is the history of production systems at Carnegie Mellon in the 1970s, where the OPS family of languages made the engine explicit, and of Forgy's doctoral work, published in 1979, which gave the field the RETE algorithm. RETE was adopted in OPS5, in CLIPS at NASA from 1985, and in the commercial shells of the 1980s, and it remains the standard matching algorithm in Drools and other modern engines. The algorithm's name comes from the Latin word for net, rete, because it builds a network of nodes that test patterns and remember the results. Its contribution was not a new kind of reasoning but a new cost for the old kind: matching became cheap enough that rule bases of thousands of rules could run in real time.

Description

The inference engine is the part of an expert system that does the reasoning: it takes the rules and the facts, finds the matches, fires the rules, and keeps going until the job is done. The hard problem was speed, because a naive engine checks every rule against every fact on every cycle, and that is far too slow for a rule base of thousands. The RETE algorithm, published by Charles Forgy in 1979, solved the problem by remembering partial matches in a network and updating them only when the facts change, so the cost of a cycle depends on what changed rather than on the size of the whole rule base. It is the reason rule engines could run at all, and it is still the core of modern business-rules engines.

The Problem

What is the inference engine of a rule-based system and the RETE algorithm that made it fast, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: compile the rules into a RETE network, with nodes that test single patterns and nodes that join patterns together. Step 2: feed the facts into the network, letting each fact flow through the nodes that test it. Step 3: remember the partial matches at each node, so a new fact only re-tests the paths it touches. Step 4: fire the rules whose patterns are fully matched, update the facts, and let the network propagate the changes. The demo runs a small worked example of the matching step: it takes five cycles with nodes visited of 120, 85, 140, 95, and 110, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the counting arithmetic, not a record of a RETE run. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"rete_year": 1979, "total_nodes_worked": 550, "mean_nodes_worked": 110.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: production systems in the 1970s, the RETE algorithm published in 1979, CLIPS from 1985, and RETE still standard in modern engines. The worked example shows the counting arithmetic: the five cycles visit 120, 85, 140, 95, and 110 nodes, for a total of 550, a mean of 110.0, a smallest of 85, and a largest of 140. The result that matters is the efficiency claim: the network visits only the nodes the changed facts touch, so the cost tracks the change rather than the rule base, and that is what made large rule bases practical. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 140, mean: 110.0, n: 5, smallest: 85, total: 550.

When It Applies

The inference engine and RETE apply wherever rules must be matched against changing facts at speed: monitoring, alarming, configuration, and business rules. They apply where the rule base is large, the changes are small, and the cost of rechecking everything would be prohibitive. They do not apply where the reasoning is goal-driven rather than data-driven, where the memory cost of the network exceeds the time saved, or where the rules themselves are the problem rather than the speed of matching them.

Limits

The limits are the limits of the algorithm itself. RETE uses memory to save time, and the network of partial matches can grow large when many facts match many patterns. It is designed for forward chaining, and it does not help a goal-driven search. It also does not solve the hard problems of expert systems: the rules still have to be written, the knowledge still has to be acquired, and the rule base still has to be maintained. RETE made the engine fast, and the hard problems stayed hard.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five cycles visit 120, 85, 140, 95, and 110 nodes, which sum to 550, and 550 divided by 5 is 110.0. The smallest cycle visits 85 nodes and the largest visits 140. These numbers describe one worked run, and they illustrate the point: the cost of a cycle is the number of nodes the changed facts touch, and that number stays small even when the rule base is large. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind RETE is the science of incremental computation: most facts do not change on most cycles, so recomputing everything is waste, and the algorithm trades memory for time by remembering partial matches. The network's nodes test patterns one at a time, and the join nodes combine the results, so a fact flows through only the tests that mention it. The science says the trade is worth it when the rule base is large and the changes are small, which is the usual case in monitoring and configuration, and the algorithm's endurance is the evidence: it has been the standard for more than forty years. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures..   • CLIPS — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CLIPS (Documents CLIPS, its 1985 NASA origin, and its rule-based design.)

History

The inference engine's history is the history of production systems at Carnegie Mellon in the 1970s, where the OPS family of languages made the engine explicit, and of Forgy's doctoral work, published in 1979, which gave the field the RETE algorithm. RETE was adopted in OPS5, in CLIPS at NASA from 1985, and in the commercial shells of the 1980s, and it remains the standard matching algorithm in Drools and other modern engines. The algorithm's name comes from the Latin word for net, rete, because it builds a network of nodes that test patterns and remember the results. Its contribution was not a new kind of reasoning but a new cost for the old kind: matching became cheap enough that rule bases of thousands of rules could run in real time.

Problem

What is the inference engine of a rule-based system and the RETE algorithm that made it fast, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

Method

Step 1: compile the rules into a RETE network, with nodes that test single patterns and nodes that join patterns together. Step 2: feed the facts into the network, letting each fact flow through the nodes that test it. Step 3: remember the partial matches at each node, so a new fact only re-tests the paths it touches. Step 4: fire the rules whose patterns are fully matched, update the facts, and let the network propagate the changes. The demo runs a small worked example of the matching step: it takes five cycles with nodes visited of 120, 85, 140, 95, and 110, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the counting arithmetic, not a record of a RETE run.

Dataset

Dataset: RETE algorithm, Charles Forgy, 1979; OPS5; CLIPS from 1985. Demo: worked example totaling network nodes visited across five cycles. Provenance: public-harvest from the cited public sources; licensing: public facts and figures, freely reusable, with sources cited. Last verified 2026-11-16.

Results reported

The checkable results are the dates: production systems in the 1970s, the RETE algorithm published in 1979, CLIPS from 1985, and RETE still standard in modern engines. The worked example shows the counting arithmetic: the five cycles visit 120, 85, 140, 95, and 110 nodes, for a total of 550, a mean of 110.0, a smallest of 85, and a largest of 140. The result that matters is the efficiency claim: the network visits only the nodes the changed facts touch, so the cost tracks the change rather than the rule base, and that is what made large rule bases practical. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 140, mean: 110.0, n: 5, smallest: 85, total: 550.

Skeptical analysis

The limits are the limits of the algorithm itself. RETE uses memory to save time, and the network of partial matches can grow large when many facts match many patterns. It is designed for forward chaining, and it does not help a goal-driven search. It also does not solve the hard problems of expert systems: the rules still have to be written, the knowledge still has to be acquired, and the rule base still has to be maintained. RETE made the engine fast, and the hard problems stayed hard.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('cycle 1', 120), ('cycle 2', 85), ('cycle 3', 140), ('cycle 4', 95), ('cycle 5', 110)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Knowledge Representation: Frames, Networks, and Ontologies JAH-ESYS-000019 · 19

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Knowledge representation is the question of how to write knowledge down so a machine can use it, and expert systems tried every answer: rules for procedures, frames for objects, semantic networks for relationships, and ontologies for the shared vocabulary of a domain. Frames, proposed by Marvin Minsky in 1974, package an object with its slots and default values, so a system knows what a typical chair or a typical disease looks like before it sees one. Semantic networks, from Ross Quillian's work in 1968, link concepts by labeled relationships, so reasoning can follow the links. Ontologies collect the vocabulary and the relationships into a shared, checkable form. The representation shapes everything else: what can be said, what can be inferred, and what the system can explain.

History

The representation line runs from Quillian's semantic networks in 1968 through Minsky's frames in 1974, the frame systems of the 1970s and 1980s, and the ontology work of the 1990s, including the Cyc project's upper ontology and the Semantic Web's OWL language, standardized in 2004. MYCIN's rules were one representation, INTERNIST's disease tree was another, and Cyc's assertions were a third, and the field learned that no single representation fits every kind of knowledge. The 1990s and 2000s made ontologies a standard engineering artifact, with shared vocabularies for medicine, biology, and manufacturing, and the modern knowledge graph descends directly from the semantic network. The lesson is that the representation is a design decision, not a discovery, and the right one depends on the knowledge.

Description

Knowledge representation is the question of how to write knowledge down so a machine can use it, and expert systems tried every answer: rules for procedures, frames for objects, semantic networks for relationships, and ontologies for the shared vocabulary of a domain. Frames, proposed by Marvin Minsky in 1974, package an object with its slots and default values, so a system knows what a typical chair or a typical disease looks like before it sees one. Semantic networks, from Ross Quillian's work in 1968, link concepts by labeled relationships, so reasoning can follow the links. Ontologies collect the vocabulary and the relationships into a shared, checkable form. The representation shapes everything else: what can be said, what can be inferred, and what the system can explain.

The Problem

What is knowledge representation in expert systems, from frames and semantic networks to ontologies, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the domain's concepts and name them, with definitions a human can check. Step 2: choose a representation for each kind of knowledge, rules for procedures, frames for objects, networks for relationships. Step 3: write the knowledge in the chosen form, linking each piece to the concepts it mentions. Step 4: test the representation by asking the questions the system must answer, and revise where it cannot answer. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five representation layers with concepts defined of 320, 480, 260, 540, and 390, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a count of a real ontology. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"frames_year": 1974, "owl_year": 2004, "total_concepts_worked": 1990, "mean_concepts_worked": 398.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: semantic networks in 1968, frames in 1974, ontology engineering in the 1990s, and OWL standardized in 2004. The worked example shows the counting arithmetic: the five layers define 320, 480, 260, 540, and 390 concepts, for a total of 1990, a mean of 398.0, a smallest of 260, and a largest of 540. The result that matters is the discipline: a representation that can be counted can be managed, and the ontologies that survived were the ones whose concepts were defined, linked, and tested. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 540, mean: 398.0, n: 5, smallest: 260, total: 1990.

When It Applies

Knowledge representation applies wherever knowledge must be explicit, shared, and auditable: medical vocabularies, manufacturing ontologies, configuration catalogues, and any domain where different systems must agree on what things mean. It applies where the concepts are stable enough to define and the definitions can be checked by experts. It does not apply where the knowledge cannot be stated, where the concepts change faster than they can be defined, or where the representation would be built and then never maintained.

Limits

The limits are the limits of explicitness. A representation captures what its authors wrote and nothing else, so the knowledge outside the representation is invisible to the system. Frames carry defaults that can be wrong, networks grow tangled as they grow large, and ontologies take years to build and maintain. The representation also shapes the reasoning: a system can only infer what its representation allows, so a bad representation limits the system more than a small rule base does. The honest account treats the representation as a bet, and judges it by whether the system can answer the questions it must answer.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five layers define 320, 480, 260, 540, and 390 concepts, which sum to 1990, and 1990 divided by 5 is 398.0. The smallest layer defines 260 concepts and the largest defines 540. These numbers describe one worked example, and they make the representation concrete: a knowledge base is a countable artifact, and its size is the first thing a maintainer needs to know. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind knowledge representation is the science of making knowledge explicit: a machine can only reason about what is written down, so the representation decides what the machine can know. Frames capture the structure of objects and their defaults, networks capture the relationships between concepts, and ontologies capture the shared vocabulary that lets different systems agree. The science says the representation must match the knowledge: rules for procedures, frames for objects, networks for relationships, and no single form for everything. The failures came from forcing one form onto knowledge it did not fit. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures..     total = round(sum(counts), 4)

Explanation Facilities: How and Why JAH-ESYS-000020 · 20

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

The explanation facility was the feature that made expert systems trustworthy: the ability to answer how a conclusion was reached and why a question was asked. When MYCIN recommended a drug, it could show the chain of rules that led there; when it asked about a symptom, it could say which rule needed the answer. That transparency was the reason clinicians took MYCIN seriously, and its absence is the reason modern learned systems are distrusted in high-stakes work. An expert system that cannot explain itself is a black box, and a black box cannot be checked, challenged, or improved.

History

Explanation facilities were part of MYCIN from the start in the 1970s, because Shortliffe and his colleagues knew that a doctor would not accept a recommendation without a reason. The facility answered two questions: how, by tracing the chain of fired rules from the facts to the conclusion, and why, by showing the rule that needed the fact being asked for. The design spread to the other systems of the era, and it became a standard requirement for expert systems in medicine, law, and finance. The idea survives in modern explainable AI, which tries to give learned systems the same transparency, and in business-rules engines, where every decision can be traced to the rules that fired. The lineage is direct, and the requirement is unchanged: a system that decides must be able to say why.

Description

The explanation facility was the feature that made expert systems trustworthy: the ability to answer how a conclusion was reached and why a question was asked. When MYCIN recommended a drug, it could show the chain of rules that led there; when it asked about a symptom, it could say which rule needed the answer. That transparency was the reason clinicians took MYCIN seriously, and its absence is the reason modern learned systems are distrusted in high-stakes work. An expert system that cannot explain itself is a black box, and a black box cannot be checked, challenged, or improved.

The Problem

What is the explanation facility of an expert system, and why showing the reasoning matters, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: record every rule that fires, with the facts that satisfied it and the conclusion it produced. Step 2: when asked how, walk the recorded chain backward from the conclusion to the facts. Step 3: when asked why, show the rule that needs the fact being requested, and the goal that rule serves. Step 4: present the chain in plain language, with the rules and the facts in order. The demo runs a small worked example of the tracing step: it takes five explanation chains with rules shown of 6, 11, 4, 9, and 7, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the counting arithmetic, not a record of a historical consultation. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_rules_shown_worked": 37, "mean_chain_worked": 7.4}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: explanation facilities in MYCIN in the 1970s, standard in the expert systems of the 1980s, and the requirement carried into modern explainable AI. The worked example shows the tracing arithmetic: the five chains show 6, 11, 4, 9, and 7 rules, for a total of 37, a mean of 7.4, a smallest of 4, and a largest of 11. The result that matters is the usability claim: a chain of seven rules can be read and checked by a human, a chain of seventy cannot, and the explanation facility is only useful when the chains it shows are short enough to understand. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 11, mean: 7.4, n: 5, smallest: 4, total: 37.

When It Applies

Explanation facilities apply wherever a system advises a human who takes responsibility: medicine, law, finance, and any high-stakes decision where the reasoning must be shown. They apply where the chains are short, the rules are readable, and the user can challenge the conclusion. They do not apply as a guarantee of correctness, they do not apply where the chains are too long to read, and they do not apply where the user has no time or authority to question what the system says.

Limits

The limits are the limits of a trace. A chain of rules shows what the system did, not whether it was right, and a wrong conclusion can have a perfectly clear trace. The trace also shows only the rules that fired, not the rules that should have fired, so a missing rule is invisible in the explanation. And a long chain is no more understandable than no chain at all, which is why the explanation facility needs the rule base to be small and the chains to be short. The honest account treats explanation as necessary but not sufficient: it lets a human check the system, and the checking is what makes the system trustworthy.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five chains show 6, 11, 4, 9, and 7 rules, which sum to 37, and 37 divided by 5 is 7.4. The shortest chain shows 4 rules and the longest shows 11. These numbers describe one worked example, and they make the design point concrete: explanations must be short enough to read, and a system whose chains run to dozens of rules has an explanation facility in name only. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind explanation is the science of trust: a user accepts a system's conclusion when the user can follow the reasoning, check the steps, and find the mistake when there is one. The how-trace gives the steps, the why-trace gives the purpose, and together they let an expert audit the system rather than merely obey it. The science says transparency is not a luxury but a requirement in high-stakes work, because the user is the one who takes responsibility for the decision, and responsibility without understanding is not responsible at all. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. History

Explanation facilities were part of MYCIN from the start in the 1970s, because Shortliffe and his colleagues knew that a doctor would not accept a recommendation without a reason. The facility answered two questions: how, by tracing the chain of fired rules from the facts to the conclusion, and why, by showing the rule that needed the fact being asked for. The design spread to the other systems of the era, and it became a standard requirement for expert systems in medicine, law, and finance. The idea survives in modern explainable AI, which tries to give learned systems the same transparency, and in business-rules engines, where every decision can be traced to the rules that fired. The lineage is direct, and the requirement is unchanged: a system that decides must be able to say why.

Problem

What is the explanation facility of an expert system, and why showing the reasoning matters, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

Method

Step 1: record every rule that fires, with the facts that satisfied it and the conclusion it produced. Step 2: when asked how, walk the recorded chain backward from the conclusion to the facts. Step 3: when asked why, show the rule that needs the fact being requested, and the goal that rule serves. Step 4: present the chain in plain language, with the rules and the facts in order. The demo runs a small worked example of the tracing step: it takes five explanation chains with rules shown of 6, 11, 4, 9, and 7, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the counting arithmetic, not a record of a historical consultation.

Dataset

Dataset: explanation facilities in MYCIN, 1970s; standard in expert systems of the 1980s. Demo: worked example totaling rules shown across five explanation chains. Provenance: public-harvest from the cited public sources; licensing: public facts and figures, freely reusable, with sources cited. Last verified 2026-11-16.

Results reported

The worked example shows the tracing arithmetic: the five chains show 6, 11, 4, 9, and 7 rules, for a total of 37, a mean of 7.4, a smallest of 4, and a largest of 11. The result that matters is the usability claim: a chain of seven rules can be read and checked by a human, a chain of seventy cannot, and the explanation facility is only useful when the chains it shows are short enough to understand. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 11, mean: 7.4, n: 5, smallest: 4, total: 37.

Skeptical analysis

The limits are the limits of a trace. A chain of rules shows what the system did, not whether it was right, and a wrong conclusion can have a perfectly clear trace. The trace also shows only the rules that fired, not the rules that should have fired, so a missing rule is invisible in the explanation. And a long chain is no more understandable than no chain at all, which is why the explanation facility needs the rule base to be small and the chains to be short. The honest account treats explanation as necessary but not sufficient: it lets a human check the system, and the checking is what makes the system trustworthy.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('chain 1', 6), ('chain 2', 11), ('chain 3', 4), ('chain 4', 9), ('chain 5', 7)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYS000020:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('chain 1', 6), ('chain 2', 11), ('chain 3', 4), ('chain 4', 9), ('chain 5', 7)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYS000020().run())

Validating an Expert System: Testing the Rule Base JAH-ESYS-000021 · 21

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

A rule base is a program, and like any program it must be tested, but testing an expert system is harder than testing ordinary software, because the specification is the expert's judgment and the judgment is not written down. Validation asks whether the system is right, by comparing its conclusions with the expert's on test cases; verification asks whether the system is built correctly, by checking the rules for consistency, completeness, and redundancy. The field learned to do both, and the lesson applies to every AI system since: a system that cannot be tested cannot be trusted, and a system whose test is its own author cannot be tested at all.

History

Validation and verification grew up with the expert systems of the 1970s and 1980s, as MYCIN's 1979 evaluation showed what a blinded comparison with experts could prove, and as the maintenance problems of XCON showed what happens when a rule base is not checked for consistency. The methods became standard: test cases drawn from real practice, blinded evaluation against experts, checks for rules that can never fire, rules that contradict each other, and gaps where no rule covers a case. The discipline survives in modern AI evaluation, where the test set must be separate from the training set and the evaluation must be blinded, and the expert-systems era is where those rules were learned.

Description

A rule base is a program, and like any program it must be tested, but testing an expert system is harder than testing ordinary software, because the specification is the expert's judgment and the judgment is not written down. Validation asks whether the system is right, by comparing its conclusions with the expert's on test cases; verification asks whether the system is built correctly, by checking the rules for consistency, completeness, and redundancy. The field learned to do both, and the lesson applies to every AI system since: a system that cannot be tested cannot be trusted, and a system whose test is its own author cannot be tested at all.

The Problem

What is validation and verification of expert systems, and how a rule base is tested, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: assemble test cases from real practice, with the expert's conclusions recorded before the system runs. Step 2: run the system on the cases and compare its conclusions with the expert's, counting agreements and disagreements. Step 3: verify the rule base itself, checking for rules that never fire, rules that contradict each other, and cases no rule covers. Step 4: return the disagreements to the expert, fix the rules, and re-test. The demo runs a small worked example of the comparison step: it takes five test batches with agreement rates of 0.9, 0.85, 0.92, 0.78, and 0.88 against reference rates of 0.9, 0.85, 0.9, 0.8, and 0.88, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the comparison arithmetic, not a record of a historical evaluation. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"evaluation_year": 1979, "mae_worked": 0.008, "rmse_worked": 0.0126, "max_gap_worked": 0.02}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the methods and the dates: MYCIN's blinded 1979 evaluation, the consistency checks developed for XCON's rule base, and the validation discipline that became standard in the 1980s. The worked example shows the comparison arithmetic: the gaps between the agreement rates and their references are 0.0, 0.0, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.0, giving a mean absolute error of 0.008 and a root mean square error of 0.0126, with a largest gap of 0.02. The result that matters is the method: a system is validated by blinded comparison with experts on real cases, and verified by checking the rule base for contradictions and gaps, and neither step can be skipped. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.008, max_err: 0.02, n: 5, rmse: 0.0126.

When It Applies

Validation applies to every rule-based system before it is used: assemble fresh test cases, evaluate blindly against experts, check the rule base for contradictions and gaps, and re-test after every change. It applies where the experts are available to judge, where the cases are real, and where the evaluation is independent of the builders. It does not apply as a one-time ceremony, it does not apply where the test cases are the training cases, and it does not apply where the judges know which answers are the machine's.

Limits

The limits are the limits of testing a system whose specification is a human judgment. The test cases must cover the hard cases, not only the easy ones, and the expert's own judgment can be wrong, so agreement with the expert is not the same as being right. Verification can find contradictions and gaps, but it cannot find a rule that is wrong in a way that is consistent with the rest of the base. And a system that passes its tests today can fail tomorrow, when the domain changes and the rules do not. The honest account tests continuously, not once.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.0, 0.0, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.0, which sum to 0.04, so the mean absolute error is 0.04 divided by 5, which is 0.008. The squared gaps are 0.0, 0.0, 0.0004, 0.0004, and 0.0, which sum to 0.0008, so the mean squared error is 0.00016 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.00016, which is 0.0126. The largest single gap is 0.02. Small errors on a worked example prove the arithmetic, not the system; the system was proved, or not, by its blinded evaluation. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind validation is the science of honest evaluation: a system tested on the cases it was built from will pass, and a system tested by its own author will pass, so the test must use fresh cases and independent judges. The blinded 1979 MYCIN evaluation is the model, because the judges did not know which recommendations were the machine's and which were the humans', and the comparison was fair. The science says the evaluation must match the use: a system that will advise clinicians must be tested on clinical cases, and a system that will configure computers must be tested on real orders, because a test on toy cases proves nothing. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.9, 0.9), (0.85, 0.85), (0.92, 0.9), (0.78, 0.8), (0.88, 0.88)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Run 2 SHA-256: e3420e07522179d72954526101a158f88e38f572ffe56c35a20f458bb42c7cf2

Modern Descendants: Business Rules Engines JAH-ESYS-000022 · 22

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

The expert system did not die in the AI winter; it changed its name and went to work. The business-rules engine is its direct descendant: a fast forward-chaining engine, a rule base written by analysts rather than programmers, and a decision service that answers the same question thousands of times a day. Drools, started in 2001 and taken up by JBoss, became the best-known open engine, and the Decision Model and Notation standard, published in 2015, gave the field a shared way to write decisions as tables. The technology survived because it does a job nothing else does as well: apply a written policy consistently, explainably, and at scale.

History

The descendant line runs from CLIPS at NASA in 1985 through the commercial shells of the 1980s, the Java engines of the 1990s and 2000s, and Drools, begun in 2001 as an open-source project and developed under JBoss and later Red Hat. The 2000s brought business-rules management systems, which let analysts write and test rules without programmers, and the 2010s brought the Decision Model and Notation standard, published by the Object Management Group in 2015, which standardized decision tables. The engines now run inside banks, insurers, retailers, and government agencies, deciding credit, pricing, eligibility, and compliance, and they run alongside learned models, with the rules constraining what the models may decide. The lineage is unbroken from MYCIN's rules to today's decision services.

Description

The expert system did not die in the AI winter; it changed its name and went to work. The business-rules engine is its direct descendant: a fast forward-chaining engine, a rule base written by analysts rather than programmers, and a decision service that answers the same question thousands of times a day. Drools, started in 2001 and taken up by JBoss, became the best-known open engine, and the Decision Model and Notation standard, published in 2015, gave the field a shared way to write decisions as tables. The technology survived because it does a job nothing else does as well: apply a written policy consistently, explainably, and at scale.

The Problem

What is business rules engines, the modern descendants of expert systems, from Drools to decision management, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: write the policy as rules or decision tables, with each rule stating a condition and an action. Step 2: load the facts of the case into the engine. Step 3: let the RETE-based engine match and fire the rules, deriving the decision. Step 4: return the decision with the rules that fired, so it can be explained and audited. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five decision services with rules fired of 22, 18, 31, 15, and 26, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the counting arithmetic, not a record of a production run. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"drools_year": 2001, "dmn_year": 2015, "total_fired_worked": 112, "mean_fired_worked": 22.4}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: CLIPS from 1985, Drools from 2001, and the Decision Model and Notation standard published in 2015. The worked example shows the counting arithmetic: the five services fire 22, 18, 31, 15, and 26 rules, for a total of 112, a mean of 22.4, a smallest of 15, and a largest of 31. The result that matters is the continuity: the engine at the heart of a modern decision service is the same forward-chaining, RETE-based design that CLIPS made public in 1985, and its survival is the evidence that the design was right. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 31, mean: 22.4, n: 5, smallest: 15, total: 112.

When It Applies

Business-rules engines apply wherever a written policy must be applied consistently, explainably, and at scale: credit decisions, pricing, eligibility, and compliance. They apply where the policy is stable enough to encode, where every decision must be auditable, and where analysts can maintain the rules. They do not apply where the pattern must be learned from data, where the policy changes faster than the rules can be revised, or where the rules would reproduce a bias nobody has audited.

Limits

The limits are the limits of policy written as rules. The rules must be maintained as the policy changes, and a rule base that is not maintained becomes a record of last year's policy. The rules cannot learn, so a new pattern is invisible until someone writes a rule for it. And the rules can reproduce a bias in the policy faithfully and at scale, which is why decision services need auditing as much as they need testing. The honest account treats the engine as infrastructure, not intelligence, and judges it by whether the policy is applied correctly and can be explained.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five services fire 22, 18, 31, 15, and 26 rules, which sum to 112, and 112 divided by 5 is 22.4. The smallest service fires 15 rules and the largest fires 31. These numbers describe one worked example, and they make the design point concrete: a decision that fires dozens of rules is auditable, and a decision that fires thousands is not, so the rule base must be kept small enough to explain. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind business-rules engines is the science of policy as code: a written policy is a set of rules, and an engine that executes the rules consistently is a policy that cannot be misapplied by a tired clerk or a forgotten memo. The engines worked because the policy was written down before the system was built, so the knowledge acquisition was transcription, and because the decisions they make are the kind that must be explained, which rules do naturally. The science says the engine's value is not intelligence but consistency and auditability, and that is a smaller claim than the 1980s made, and a truer one. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('service 1', 22), ('service 2', 18), ('service 3', 31), ('service 4', 15), ('service 5', 26)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYS000022:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('service 1', 22), ('service 2', 18), ('service 3', 31), ('service 4', 15), ('service 5', 26)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYS000022().run())

The Limits of Expert Systems: Brittleness and Maintenance JAH-ESYS-000023 · 23

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Every expert system has the same limits, and they are the reason the boom ended: brittleness, the tendency to fail suddenly and completely outside its narrow scope; maintenance cost, the endless work of keeping thousands of rules correct as the world changes; and the knowledge-acquisition bottleneck, the cost of extracting expertise that was never written down. A system that knows about bacterial infections knows nothing about a broken leg, and it does not know that it knows nothing: it will answer confidently or not at all, and neither is acceptable. These limits are not bugs to be fixed but properties of the approach, and the honest account of expert systems starts with them.

History

The limits were visible from the start and documented through the 1980s, as XCON's rule base grew to about 2,500 rules and its maintenance became a job in itself, as Cyc's millions of assertions showed how slowly hand-built knowledge accumulates, and as the AI winter showed what happens when the maintenance bill arrives. Brittleness was demonstrated whenever a system met a case outside its rules: it had no common sense to notice, no learning to adapt, and no graceful way to say it did not know. The field's response was not to deny the limits but to narrow the ambition: build systems for closed, stable, documented domains, maintain them continuously, and keep a human in the loop. That response is the whole of the modern practice of rule-based systems.

Description

Every expert system has the same limits, and they are the reason the boom ended: brittleness, the tendency to fail suddenly and completely outside its narrow scope; maintenance cost, the endless work of keeping thousands of rules correct as the world changes; and the knowledge-acquisition bottleneck, the cost of extracting expertise that was never written down. A system that knows about bacterial infections knows nothing about a broken leg, and it does not know that it knows nothing: it will answer confidently or not at all, and neither is acceptable. These limits are not bugs to be fixed but properties of the approach, and the honest account of expert systems starts with them.

The Problem

What is the limits of expert systems, brittleness, maintenance cost, and narrow scope, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take a system and test it on cases inside its scope, recording where it succeeds. Step 2: test it on cases just outside its scope, and record how it fails. Step 3: count the maintenance work, the rules changed per year and the effort per change. Step 4: report the scope, the failure mode, and the maintenance cost together, because a system is only as good as all three. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five maintenance years with rules changed of 180, 240, 150, 310, and 210, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a record of a historical maintenance log. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_changed_worked": 1090, "mean_changed_worked": 218.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the scale figures: about 600 rules for MYCIN, about 2,500 for XCON, millions of assertions for Cyc, and the maintenance burden that grew with each. The worked example shows the counting arithmetic: the five years change 180, 240, 150, 310, and 210 rules, for a total of 1090, a mean of 218.0, a smallest of 150, and a largest of 310. The result that matters is the rate: at more than two hundred rules changed a year, a rule base is a living artifact that needs a staff, not a finished product that can be left alone. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 310, mean: 218.0, n: 5, smallest: 150, total: 1090.

When It Applies

The limits apply as a design discipline: before building a rule-based system, name the scope, name the failure mode outside it, and budget the maintenance. They apply to every expert system, from MYCIN to the newest decision service, and they apply to the maintenance as much as to the building. They do not apply as a verdict that rule-based systems are useless, because inside their scope they are the most reliable and explainable systems there are, and the discipline is what keeps them inside it.

Limits

The limits are the whole subject of this record, so they are stated plainly. Expert systems are brittle outside their scope, expensive to maintain, unable to learn, and silent about what they do not know. They cannot handle a case their authors did not foresee, they cannot adapt to a world that changes faster than their rules, and they cannot be trusted without a human checking them. The honest account does not hide these limits behind the successes: the successes happened inside narrow, stable, documented domains, and the limits define the edge of those domains.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five years change 180, 240, 150, 310, and 210 rules, which sum to 1090, and 1090 divided by 5 is 218.0. The smallest year changes 150 rules and the largest changes 310. These numbers describe one worked example, and they make the maintenance cost concrete: a rule base is not built once but maintained forever, and the maintenance is measured in rules changed per year. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind the limits is the science of closed worlds: a rule-based system works inside a world its authors described, and it has no way to know when the world has moved on. Brittleness follows from the representation: a system that reasons by matching rules has no gradient of confidence, so it is right or it is silent, and its silence is indistinguishable from its ignorance. Maintenance follows from the same cause: the world changes, the rules do not, and the gap between them grows until someone closes it by hand. The science says these are properties of the approach, not failures of implementation, and the remedy is to choose domains where the world changes slowly and the scope is closed. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYS000023:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('year 1', 180), ('year 2', 240), ('year 3', 150), ('year 4', 310), ('year 5', 210)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYS000023().run())

Knowledge Engineering: The Discipline Behind the Rule Base JAH-ESYS-000024 · 24

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Knowledge engineering is the discipline of building the knowledge base of an expert system, and it is the work that made the classic systems possible. The term was coined by Edward Feigenbaum in 1977, and the discipline covers the whole craft: working with a human expert, drawing out what the expert knows, writing it down as rules, frames, or networks, testing it against cases, and keeping it up to date. The knowledge engineer is the person who does this work, standing between the expert and the machine, and the quality of the system depends on the quality of that work more than on any other single factor.

History

The discipline began with the first expert systems themselves. DENDRAL in the 1960s and MYCIN in the 1970s showed that a machine could reason like an expert if it was given the expert's knowledge in a form it could use, and the question of how to get that knowledge out of the expert became a field of its own. Feigenbaum named the field in his 1977 paper, The Art of Artificial Intelligence, which presented DENDRAL and MYCIN as case studies of knowledge engineering. Randall Davis built TEIRESIAS in 1976, a program that helped MYCIN's developers add and test new rules by talking to the system in English, and William van Melle extracted EMYCIN from MYCIN in 1979 and 1980, the first expert-system shell, a MYCIN with the medicine taken out. The 1980s brought commercial shells such as KEE, ART, and Nexpert Object, and with them a profession: knowledge engineers who built systems for companies that had never had one. The discipline's central problem, the knowledge acquisition bottleneck, was named in the same period, and it has never been fully solved.

Description

Knowledge engineering is the discipline of building the knowledge base of an expert system, and it is the work that made the classic systems possible. The term was coined by Edward Feigenbaum in 1977, and the discipline covers the whole craft: working with a human expert, drawing out what the expert knows, writing it down as rules, frames, or networks, testing it against cases, and keeping it up to date. The knowledge engineer is the person who does this work, standing between the expert and the machine, and the quality of the system depends on the quality of that work more than on any other single factor.

The Problem

What is knowledge engineering, the discipline of building expert-system knowledge bases, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: elicit the knowledge, by interviewing the expert, by watching the expert work, by protocol analysis of the expert thinking aloud, and by card sorting and laddering to find the structure of the expert's categories. Step 2: represent the knowledge, as production rules where the knowledge is conditional, as frames where it is structured, or as semantic networks where it is relational, choosing the form that fits the knowledge rather than forcing the knowledge into one form. Step 3: validate the knowledge, by running the system on test cases, by checking the explanations it gives, and by asking the expert whether the system's reasoning matches their own. Step 4: maintain the knowledge, by adding rules as the domain changes and removing the ones that no longer hold. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five elicitation sessions that captured 12, 18, 9, 22, and 15 rules, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the discipline's arithmetic, and its counts are illustrative, not measurements of any real project. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"rules_captured_total": 76, "mean_rules_per_session": 15.2, "term_coined_year": 1977}.

The Answer

The worked example's counts are 12, 18, 9, 22, and 15 rules captured across five elicitation sessions, for a total of 76 rules, a mean of 15.2 rules per session, a smallest session of 9 rules, and a largest of 22. The result that matters is not the count but the judgment it supports: a discipline that measures its elicitation, the rules captured per session, the agreement between experts, and the validation pass rate, can improve, and one that does not measure is guessing. The history supports the same judgment: the systems that survived, MYCIN's descendants and the commercial shells, were the ones whose knowledge was elicited carefully and validated against cases, and the systems that failed were often the ones whose knowledge was gathered hastily and never tested. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 22, mean: 15.2, n: 5, smallest: 9, total: 76.

When It Applies

Knowledge engineering applies wherever an expert system or a knowledge base must be built: it is the discipline of getting the knowledge right before the machine is asked to reason with it. It applies to medicine, law, finance, and industry, and to the modern descendants of expert systems, the business rules engines and decision systems that still need their rules written by someone who understands the domain. It applies as a craft with named methods, named tools, and measurable outcomes, and it does not apply as a shortcut: there is no way to build a knowledge base without doing the elicitation, and the systems that skip it are the ones that fail.

Limits

The limits of knowledge engineering are the limits of the expert and of the elicitation. The expert may be wrong, and the knowledge engineer who writes down a wrong rule faithfully has built a faithful copy of a mistake. The expert may disagree with other experts, and the system must then choose, or carry both views and say so. The knowledge ages, and a rule base that is not maintained rots, which is why the maintenance step is part of the discipline rather than an afterthought. The bottleneck is the deepest limit: the elicitation is slow, the expert's time is expensive, and there has never been a method that extracts knowledge as fast as a system can use it.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five elicitation sessions captured 12, 18, 9, 22, and 15 rules, which sum to 76, and 76 divided by 5 is 15.2. The smallest session captured 9 rules and the largest captured 22. The spread between the smallest and the largest, 13 rules, is itself a finding: the sessions varied widely, which is what a real elicitation looks like, because some sessions reach the expert's core knowledge and some only circle it. These are illustrative counts, and their transparency is the point: a record whose arithmetic can be checked is a record that can be argued with. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science of knowledge engineering is the science of tacit knowledge. The philosopher Michael Polanyi wrote that we know more than we can tell, and the expert is the proof: a doctor who can diagnose at a glance often cannot say how, and a geologist who can read a rock often cannot list the rules. Elicitation methods exist because of this gap: the interview asks directly, the protocol analysis catches the expert in the act of thinking, and the card sort reveals categories the expert never named. The representation matters too, because the form shapes what can be said: a rule says when to act, a frame says what a thing is, and a network says how things relate, and the knowledge engineer must choose among them. The science is honest about its limits, and the bottleneck is its central finding, that getting the knowledge out is harder than using it once it is out. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('session 1', 12), ('session 2', 18), ('session 3', 9), ('session 4', 22), ('session 5', 15)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
